Appendix
A
discusses the category of orbifolds via Lie groupoids and Morita equivalence. This categorical perspective allows to rigorously define orbifolds as stacky quotients, encoding both the local structure of singularities (via isotropy groups) and their global topological behaviour. The notion of stratification by isotropy type is introduced to describe the singular part of an orbifold as a union of smooth open subspaces. Further, the theory of
G
-structures to orbifolds is discussed. In particular, the focus lies on reductions of the frame bundle to subgroups
G\subset\mathrm{GL}(n)
, with special emphasise on
\mathrm{Spin}(7)
-structures. The torsion of such structures will be discussed as it plays a central role as an obstruction to special holonomy metrics.

2.
Spin(7)-Structures on Orbifolds

We will begin with a brief introduction into octonionic linear algebra and geometry of spaces (manifolds, orbifolds) with holonomy
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
. As the construction of manifolds with holonomy
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
by resolving orbifolds is the main goal of this paper, these definitions will be essential. For those readers with a background in the geometry of special holonomy manifolds it is still recommended to read the next two sections as notations and conventions may differ form other authors. More elaborated versions of the following discussion can be found in the work of Joyce
[
Joy00
, Sec. 10]
, Salamon and Walpuski in
[
SW10
, Sec. 5-7, Sec. 9]
.

2.1.
Octonionic Linear Algebra and Spin(7)

The octonions are a nonassociative skew-field extension of
\mathbb{R}
and extend the sequence

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{C}\subset\mathbb{H}

of real normed division algebra. Their complex intrinsic algebraic structure can be encompassed in various tensors. For instance, the inner product is given by

\displaystyle\left<u,v\right>_{0}={\mathrm{Re}}(\overline{u}v).

Here
{\mathrm{Re}}(\mathbb{O})=\mathbb{R}\cdot 1=C(\mathbb{O})
. Further, we define the triple cross-product

\displaystyle u\times_{0}v\times_{0}w\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2}(u(\overline{v}w)-w(\overline{v}u)).

This triple-cross product can be seen as a homomorphism

\displaystyle.\times_{0}.\times_{0}.\colon\wedge^{3}\mathbb{O}^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{O}

and is used to define the standard Cayley form
\Phi_{0}\in\wedge^{4}\mathbb{O}^{*}
by

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}(x,y,z,w)\coloneqq

\displaystyle\left<x,y\times_{0}z\times_{0}w\right>_{0}.

It is usually more helpful to think of a Cayley form as an abstract four form over an eight-dimensional spaces, rather then the coordinate expression over
\mathbb{O}\cong\mathbb{R}^{8}
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
Cayley form

\Phi
on a eight-dimensional vector space
W
is a four form that is given by

\displaystyle\Phi=i^{*}\Phi_{0}

for an isomorphism
i:W\rightarrow\mathbb{O}
.

\lozenge

Hence, we identify the space of Cayley forms on
(W,\Phi)
with the
{\mathrm{GL}}(W)
orbit of
\Phi

\displaystyle Cay

\displaystyle=\{A^{*}\Phi|A\in{\mathrm{GL}}(W)\}.

We will summarise some well known facts about the group
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
following
[
Joy00
, Sec. 10]
and
[
SW10
, Sec. 5-7, Sec. 9]
.

•

{\mathrm{Stab}}(\Phi)\cong{\mathrm{Stab}}(\Phi_{0})={\mathrm{Spin}}(7)

•

Cay\cong{\mathrm{GL}}(8;\mathbb{R})/{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)

•

Cay=Cay_{+}\cup Cay_{-}
, where elements of
Cay_{+}
are referred to as positive Cayley forms.

•

Let
\Phi\in Cay_{+}
. As representation of
{\mathrm{Stab}}(\Phi)
the following irreducible
{\mathrm{GL}}(W)
-representations decompose into
\wedge^{2}W^{*}\cong\wedge^{2}_{7,\Phi}\oplus\wedge^{2}_{21,\Phi}
,
\wedge^{3}W^{*}\cong\wedge^{3}_{8,\Phi}\oplus\wedge^{3}_{48,\Phi}
,
\wedge^{4}W^{*}\cong\wedge^{4}_{1,\Phi}\oplus\wedge^{4}_{7,\Phi}\oplus\wedge^{4}_{28,\Phi}\oplus\wedge^{4}_{35,\Phi}

•

If
\Phi\in Cay_{+}
than
\Phi\in\wedge^{4}_{+,\Phi}
.

•

T_{\Phi}Cay_{+}\cong\wedge^{4}_{1,\Phi}\oplus\wedge^{4}_{7,\Phi}\oplus\wedge^{4}_{35,\Phi}

•

There exists a fibration
\gamma:Cay\rightarrow Met(8)
, where
Met(8)={\mathrm{GL}}(8;\mathbb{R})/{\mathrm{SO}}(8)
is the space of inner product, whose fibres are isomorphic to
\mathbb{RP}^{7}\cong{\mathrm{SO}}(8)/{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
.

2.2.
The Tubular Neighbourhood of Cayley Forms

In contrast to the orbit of positive three-forms in
\mathrm{Im}(\mathbb{O})
, i.e. the space of associative calibrations, the space of positive Cayley calibrations is not an open
\mathrm{GL}_{+}(8,\mathbb{R})
-orbit in the space of four-forms, but rather a submanifold of codimension 28. Consequently, generic small perturbations of a Cayley calibration in the space of four-forms do not remain within the space
Cay
. To compensate for this lack of openness, we construct a
\mathrm{GL}_{+}(W)
-equivariant tubular neighbourhood, which allows us to project a generically perturbed Cayley calibration back onto the space of positive Cayley forms.

Let
j\colon Cay_{+}\hookrightarrow\wedge^{4}W^{*}
be the inclusion and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TCay_{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}T\wedge^{4}W^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces NCay_{+}}

be the induced exact sequence. Since both
TCay_{+}
and
j^{*}T\wedge^{4}W^{*}
can be equipped with a metric, by

\displaystyle G(v,w)=g_{\Phi}(v,w)\hskip 28.45274pt\text{for all }v,w\in\wedge^{4}W^{*}

we obtain an orthogonal splitting
j^{*}T\wedge^{4}W^{*}\cong TCay_{+}\oplus NCay_{+}
. Let us define the set
Tub_{\rho}(Cay_{+})\subset j^{*}T\wedge^{4}W^{*}=\{v\in NCay_{+}\colon\left|\left|v\right|\right|<\rho\}
. Using parallel-transport by translation we can define

\displaystyle{\mathrm{exp}}:Tub_{\rho}(Cay_{+})\hookrightarrow\wedge^{4}W^{*}.

For small enough
\rho
this map is a diffeomorphism. We denote the projection map by

\displaystyle\Theta:Tub_{\rho}(Cay_{+})\rightarrow Cay_{+}.

Lemma 2.1
.

[
Joy00
, Prop. 10.5.9]

Both
Tub_{\rho}(Cay_{+})
and
Cay_{+}
are
{\mathrm{GL}}_{+}(W)
-spaces and the map
\Theta
is
{\mathrm{GL}}_{+}(W)
-equivariant. Given a
\Phi\in Cay_{+}
and
\Phi+\eta\in{\mathrm{Tub}}_{\rho}(Cay_{+})
, we can write

(4)

\displaystyle\Theta(\Phi+\eta)=

\displaystyle\Phi+\pi_{\tau,\Phi}(\eta)+Q_{\Phi}(\eta).

Here linear part is given by

\displaystyle\pi_{\tau,\Phi}(\eta)=\pi_{1,\Phi}(\eta)+\pi_{7,\Phi}(\eta)+\pi_{35,\Phi}(\eta)

with respect to
\Phi
and
Q_{\Phi}(\eta)
satisfies

\displaystyle|Q_{\Phi}(\eta)-Q_{\Phi}(\eta^{\prime})|_{g_{\Phi}}\lesssim

\displaystyle|\eta-\eta^{\prime}|_{g_{\Phi}}\left(|\eta|_{g_{\Phi}}+|\eta^{\prime}|_{g_{\Phi}}\right).

2.3.
Finite Symmetries of Cayley Forms

Throughout this paper we will encounter various finite subgroups preserving a fixed Cayley structure, all of which fall into the following framework.

Let
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Spin}(7)
be a finite subgroup acting naturally on
W\cong\mathbb{O}
, and let

\displaystyle H\coloneqq W^{\Gamma}

denote the fixed subspace under this action. Then, by construction,

\displaystyle\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{ker}}\left(\mathrm{Spin}(7)\left(W\to H\right)\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{SO}(H)\right).

This condition is very restrictive, and hence such subgroups are necessarily of one of the following types:

(1)

{\mathrm{dim}}(H)=8
,
\Gamma={1}
and
W/\Gamma\cong W
.

(2)

{\mathrm{dim}}(H)=4
,
\Gamma
is conjugate to a finite subgroup of
\mathrm{Sp}(1)
, and
W/\Gamma\cong\mathbb{R}^{4}\times(\mathbb{H}/\Gamma)
.

(3)

{\mathrm{dim}}(H)=2
,
\Gamma
is conjugate to a finite subgroup of
\mathrm{SU}(3)
, and
W/\Gamma\cong\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\mathbb{C}^{3}/\Gamma)
.

(4)

{\mathrm{dim}}(H)=1
,
\Gamma
is conjugate to a finite subgroup of
G_{2}
, and
W/\Gamma\cong\mathbb{R}\times(\mathrm{Im},\mathbb{O}/\Gamma)
.

(5)

{\mathrm{dim}}(H)=0
.

We will continue by describing how
\Gamma
-invariant Cayley forms can be written in terms of data on
H
and
H^{\perp}=V
.

Case (2):
Let
H
and
V
be four dimensional real vector spaces. A
\Gamma
-invariant Cayley form
\Phi
decomposes into

\displaystyle\Phi=\Phi^{4,0}+\Phi^{2,2}+\Phi^{0,4}\in\wedge^{4}H^{*}\oplus\wedge^{2}H^{*}\otimes\wedge^{2}V^{*}\oplus\wedge^{4}V^{*}

The tensor
\Phi^{2,2}\in\wedge^{2}H^{*}\otimes\wedge^{2}V^{*}
is bi-self-dual with respect to volume forms
\Phi^{4,0}\in\wedge^{4}H^{*}
and
\Phi^{0,4}\in\wedge^{4}V^{*}
, and an isomorphism
\phi\colon\wedge^{4}H^{*}\rightarrow\wedge^{4}H
. The map

\displaystyle\Phi^{2,2}\colon\wedge^{2}H\rightarrow\wedge^{2}V^{*}

defines an isomorphism of maximal positive subspaces. Three out of four tensors determine the fourth and we will write

\displaystyle\Phi={\mathrm{vol}}_{H}-{\mathrm{tr}}_{+}(\underline{\omega}_{H}\wedge\underline{\omega}_{V})+{\mathrm{vol}}_{V}.

Here,
{\mathrm{tr}}_{+}(\underline{\omega}_{H}\wedge\underline{\omega}_{V})
denotes the trace of the identification
\Phi\colon\wedge^{2}_{+}H\rightarrow\wedge^{2}_{+}V^{*}
. We view
\underline{\omega}_{H}\in\wedge^{2}_{+}H^{*}\otimes{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{H})
and
\underline{\omega}_{V}\in\wedge^{2}_{+}V^{*}\otimes{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{H})
as
{\mathrm{Sp}}(1)
-structures on
H
and
V
respectively.

Case (3):
Let
H
be two dimensional and
V
six dimensional real vector spaces. In this case a
\Gamma
-invariant Cayley form
\Phi
decomposes into

\displaystyle\Phi=\Phi^{2,2}+\Phi^{1,3}+\Phi^{0,4}\in\wedge^{2}H^{*}\otimes\wedge^{2}V^{*}\oplus\wedge^{1}H^{*}\otimes\wedge^{3}V^{*}\oplus\wedge^{4}V^{*}

Such a
\Gamma
-invariant Cayley form
\Phi
is determined by an
z\in\wedge^{1,0}_{\mathbb{C}}H^{*}
and a
{\mathrm{SU}}(3)
-structure
(\omega,\theta)\in\wedge^{2}V^{*}\oplus\wedge^{3,0}_{\mathbb{C}}V^{*}
on
V
and we write

\displaystyle\Phi=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}(z\wedge\theta-\overline{z}\wedge\overline{\theta})+z\wedge\overline{z}\wedge\omega+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega\wedge\omega

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{0}\wedge{\mathrm{Re}}(\theta)-e^{1}\wedge{\mathrm{Im}}(\theta)+e^{0}\wedge e^{1}\wedge\omega+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega\wedge\omega.

Case (4):
Let
H
be a one dimensional and
V
a seven dimensional real vector space. A
\Gamma
-invariant Cayley form
\Phi
decomposes into

\displaystyle\Phi=\Phi^{1,3}+\Phi^{0,4}\in\wedge^{1}H^{*}\otimes\wedge^{3}V^{*}\oplus\wedge^{4}V^{*}

Such a
\Gamma
-invariant Cayley form
\Phi
is determined by a linear map

\displaystyle\Phi^{1,3}:H\rightarrow\wedge^{3}V^{*}

such that
\Phi^{1,3}(e)=\varphi
defines
{\mathrm{G}}_{2}
structures for
e\neq 0
. Further we will write

\displaystyle\Phi=e\wedge\varphi+*_{\varphi}\varphi.

2.4.
Spin(7)-Orbifolds

The following section collects the necessary background on
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifolds, including their structure as spaces with a closed Cayley form, the induced spinorial interpretation, and the main properties of their moduli space.

Let
X
be an eight dimensional, real, connected, oriented and spinnable orbifold and
Fr_{+}(X)
its
{\mathrm{GL}}_{+}(8)
-frame bundle.

Definition 2.2
.

The bundle of
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures of positive Cayley forms is given by

\displaystyle Cay_{+}(X)\coloneqq Fr_{+}(X)/{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)\subset\wedge^{4}T^{*}X

The projection map

\displaystyle\gamma:Cay_{+}(X)\twoheadrightarrow Met(X)

assigns to a Cayley form
\Phi\in\Gamma(X,Cay_{+}(X))
a corresponding Riemannian metric
g_{\Phi}
. The
{\mathrm{GL}}_{+}(8)
-equivariant tubular neighbourhood

\displaystyle\Theta:Tub_{\rho}(Cay_{+})\rightarrow Cay_{+}

induces an open subset

\displaystyle Fr_{+}(X)\times_{{\mathrm{GL}}_{+}(8)}Tub_{\rho}(Cay_{+})\subset\wedge^{4}T^{*}X

of four forms that are projectable onto the bundle of Cayley forms.

\lozenge

The following result by
[
Fer86
]
proves that the
1
-flatness of
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure is equivalent to the Cayley form being closed.

Theorem 2.1
.

[
Fer86
, Thm. 5.3]
[
Bry87
, Thm. 2]

A
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure is 1-flat, i.e.
T(\Phi)=0
if and only if

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\Phi=0.

Remark 1
.

Recall that
\Phi\in\Gamma(X,Cay_{+}(X))
implies that
\Phi
is a self-dual four form with respect to the metric
g_{\Phi}
. In the spirit of this paper it is of more geometric meaning to think of a torsion free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure as one that is harmonic

(5)

\displaystyle D_{\Phi}\Phi=0

with respect to
D_{\Phi}={\mathrm{d}}+{\mathrm{d}}^{*_{\Phi}}
.

Notation
.

In this paper we will refer to an orbifold
(X,\Phi)
with a torsion-free, i.e. 1-flat
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
as a
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold.

Let
\mathbb{S}^{+}
and
\mathbb{S}^{-}
denote the two irreducible spinorial
{\mathrm{Spin}}(8)
-representations and
W
the vector representation.

Figure 2.
The Dynkin diagram of
\mathfrak{spin}(8)
, folded to the one of
\mathfrak{spin}(7)
by
\mathbb{S}^{-}\cong W
or folded to
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
by
\mathbb{S}^{+}\cong\mathbb{S}^{-}\cong W
.

Let
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)\hookrightarrow{\mathrm{Spin}}(8)
be the subgroup, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{+}\cong\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathfrak{spin}(7)^{\perp}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\mathbb{S}^{-}\cong W.

Let further

\displaystyle\widetilde{Fr}(X)\rightarrow Met(X)

denote the universal
{\mathrm{Spin}}(8)
-bundle over
Met(X)
.
6
6

6

Here we assumed that
w_{1}(X)=0
and
w_{2}(X)
.
We define the universal spinor bundles

\displaystyle SX\cong S^{+}X\oplus S^{-}X\rightarrow Met(X)

by the associated bundle construction. Now, given a
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure, we are able to associate a non-vanishing spinor

\displaystyle\Phi\in\Gamma(X,Cay(X))\rightsquigarrow s_{\Phi}\in\Gamma(X,S^{+}_{\Phi}X/\mathbb{Z}_{2})

up to a sign.

Lemma 2.2
.

[
Got02
, Chap. 7]

Let
\Phi\in\Gamma(X,Cay(X))
, then

\displaystyle S^{+}_{\Phi}X\cong\wedge^{4}_{1,\Phi}T^{*}X\oplus\wedge^{4}_{7;\Phi}T^{*}X\hskip 28.45274ptS^{-}_{\Phi}X\cong\wedge^{5}_{8,\Phi}T^{*}X

where
\mathbb{R}\cdot s_{\Phi}\cong\mathbb{R}\cdot\Phi=\wedge^{4}_{1,\Phi}T^{*}X
. Moreover, the correspondence between
s_{\Phi}
and
\Phi
is given by

\displaystyle 1+\Phi+14\cdot{\mathrm{vol}}_{\Phi}=s_{\Phi}\otimes s_{\Phi}.

Proposition 2.1
.

[
Joy00
, Prop. 10.5.6]

A
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure
\Phi
is torsion-free if and only if

\displaystyle\nabla^{\Phi}s_{\Phi}=0

where
\nabla^{\Phi}
denotes the lift of the Levi-Civita connection to the positive spinor bundle. The existence of a parallel non-vanishing spinor implies that
(X,g_{\Phi})
is Ricci-flat.

Hence, spinorial geometry and the special holonomy geometry are deeply connected. Moreover, the next proposition yields a relation of the index of the positive spin Dirac operator
\not{D}^{+}_{\Phi}
on a closed manifold and the holonomy group of
g_{\Phi}
.

Proposition 2.2
.

[
Joy00
, Thm. 10.6.1]

Let
(X,\Phi)
be a
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-manifold. The
\hat{A}
-genus of
X
is given by

\displaystyle 24\hat{A}(X)=-1+b_{1}(X)-b_{2}(X)+b_{3}(X)+b_{4}^{+}(X)-2b_{4}^{-}(X).

If
\hat{A}(X)=1
the holonomy of
(X,\Phi)
is equal to
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
, if
\hat{A}(X)=2
the holonomy of
(X,\Phi)
is equal to
{\mathrm{SU}}(4)
, if
\hat{A}(X)=3
the holonomy of
(X,\Phi)
is equal to
{\mathrm{Sp}}(2)
and if
\hat{A}(X)=4
the holonomy of
(X,\Phi)
is equal to
{\mathrm{Spin}}(4)
.

Corollary 2.1
.

Every compact, connected Riemannian eight manifold with holonomy equal to
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
is simply connected, i.e.
\pi_{1}(X)=0
.

2.5.
The Moduli Space of Torsion-Free Spin(7)-Structures

At the heart of exceptional holonomy geometry lies the study of moduli spaces of torsion-free structures. These moduli spaces encode not only the local deformation theory of special holonomy metrics but also their global behaviour under degeneration. A fundamental feature, already visible in Joyce’s work, is that orbifolds naturally appear at the boundary of such moduli spaces. One of the principal aims of this paper is to develop a general resolution scheme that allows one to pass from singular orbifold points back into the smooth part of the moduli space, thereby clarifying the interaction between smooth and singular geometries in the study of exceptional holonomy spaces.

Definition 2.3
.

The
moduli space of Spin(7)-manifolds
is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Spin}(7)\coloneqq

\displaystyle\left\{(X,\Phi)|\Phi\in\Gamma(X,Cay_{+}(X)),D_{\Phi}\Phi=0\right\}/\textbf{Diff}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{(X,\Phi)|\Phi\in\Gamma(X,Cay_{+}(X)),{\mathrm{d}}\Phi=0\right\}/\textbf{Diff}_{0}.

Here
\textbf{Diff}_{0}
denote diffeomorphisms that are isotopic to the identity.

\lozenge

A fundamental feature of the moduli space is that the deformation theory of torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures is governed by an elliptic and unobstructed deformation complex. Infinitesimal deformations are described by certain harmonic four-forms, and the linearisation of the torsion-free condition yields an elliptic operator. Moreover, this deformation complex can be naturally extended to an elliptic complex
[
Got02
, Thm. 7.2]
which folds into a twisted Dirac operator. This ellipticity ensures that the moduli space is finite-dimensional manifold.

Theorem 2.2
.

[
Joy96a
, Thm. D]

The moduli space
\mathfrak{Spin}(7)
is a smooth manifold. Its connected components have the dimension

\displaystyle{\mathrm{dim}}(\mathfrak{Spin}(7)[X])=\hat{A}(X)+b_{1}(X)+b^{4}_{-}(X).

It is endowed with a natural Riemannian metric given by the
L^{2}
-metric on
T_{[\Phi]}\mathfrak{Spin}(7)\cong\mathcal{H}^{4}_{1;\Phi}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{4}_{7;\Phi}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{4}_{35;\Phi}
.

A natural expectation, supported by Joyce’s foundational work, is that
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifolds necessarily arise in the boundary of the moduli space of smooth
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-manifolds. Indeed, Joyce’s resolution constructions begin precisely with compact
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifolds with isolated singularities and produce nearby smooth
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-manifolds by gluing in (trivial) asymptotically conically fibred spaces. This shows not only that orbifolds occur as degeneration limits of smooth metrics, but also that they can sometimes be desingularised within the category of
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-manifolds.

This raises the broader question of how the moduli space of
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifolds, which we denote by
\mathfrak{Spin}(7)^{{\mathrm{orb}}}
, interacts with the smooth moduli space
\mathfrak{Spin}(7)
. The inclusion

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Spin}(7)\subset\mathfrak{Spin}(7)^{{\mathrm{orb}}}

is tautological, but the size of the difference between these two spaces is not well understood. In particular, one would like to know under what conditions an orbifold point in
\mathfrak{Spin}(7)^{\mathrm{orb}}
can be resolved to a smooth point of
\mathfrak{Spin}(7)
. Joyce’s constructions establish existence results in specific settings, but a general criterion ensuring resolvability of
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifolds remains elusive.

From the metric viewpoint, smooth
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
resolutions of orbifold singularities can be interpreted as producing paths in the Gromov–Hausdorff topology that lead from orbifold boundary points back into the interior of
\mathfrak{Spin}(7)
. In Section
3.1
we will introduce the notion of smooth Gromov–Hausdorff resolutions, which formalises this idea. These should be seen as the geometric manifestation of a “smooth path into the bulk” of the moduli space, connecting singular boundary strata to families of smooth
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-metrics.

Figure 3.
A sketch of the moduli space of torsion-free Spin(7) structures. The finite
L^{2}
-distance boundary strata (red) are given by Spin(7)-orbifolds. The green path visualises a smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolution.

Thus, one of the central problems in understanding the global structure of
\mathfrak{Spin}(7)
is to determine to what extent the boundary of the moduli space is exhausted by orbifolds, and when such orbifolds can be resolved. This perspective situates
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-geometry firmly within the broader paradigm of special holonomy moduli problems, where singular degenerations and their resolutions play a decisive role in shaping the global geometry of the moduli space.

2.6.
The Spin(7)-Deformation Problem

In the following we will discuss how a
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure
\Phi^{pre}
with a “small” torsion can be deformed to a torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure.

The map

\displaystyle T^{*}X\rightarrow{\mathrm{End}}(\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X),\xi\mapsto\left(\omega\mapsto\xi\wedge\omega-\iota_{\xi^{\sharp_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}}}\omega\right)

satisfies the Clifford property and hence, induces a faithfully representation of

\displaystyle{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}\colon{\mathrm{Cl}}(T^{*}X,g_{\Phi^{pre}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{so}(\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X,g_{\Phi^{pre}})\subset{\mathrm{End}}(T^{*}X)

which is skew-Hermitian with respect to
g_{\Phi^{pre}}
. There exists an involutive isometry

\displaystyle\epsilon_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}=i^{(\bullet(\bullet-1))}*_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}\colon\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X\rightarrow T^{*}X

such that
\{{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}},\epsilon_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}\}=0
, and

\displaystyle\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X=\wedge^{\bullet}_{+_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}}T^{*}X\oplus\wedge^{\bullet}_{-_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}}T^{*}X.

Hence, the tuple

\displaystyle(\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X,{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}},g_{\Phi^{pre}},\nabla^{g_{\Phi^{pre}}},\epsilon_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}})

forms a graded Hermitian Dirac bundle. Its Dirac operator

\displaystyle D_{\Phi^{pre}}:={\mathrm{d}}+{\mathrm{d}}^{*_{\Phi^{pre}}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}&D^{-}_{\Phi^{pre}}\\
D_{\Phi^{pre}}^{+}&\end{array}\right)

and
D^{+}_{\Phi^{pre}}
is the signature operator of
(X,\Phi^{pre})
. Notice that the skew-adjoint “opposite” signature operator, an operator that will be important in the deformation of
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures decomposes with respect to
\epsilon_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}
into

\displaystyle D_{\Phi^{pre}}^{op}\coloneqq{\mathrm{d}}-{\mathrm{d}}^{*_{\Phi^{pre}}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}D^{op;-}_{\Phi^{pre}}&\\
&D^{op;+}_{\Phi^{pre}}\end{array}\right)

and hence,

\displaystyle D_{\Phi^{pre}}\pi_{\pm,\Phi^{pre}}=\pi_{\pm,\Phi^{pre}}D_{\Phi^{pre}}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274ptD_{\Phi^{pre}}^{op}\pi_{\pm;\Phi^{pre}}=\pi_{\mp;\Phi^{pre}}D_{\Phi^{pre}}^{op}.

This operator squares to
-\Delta_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}
and anti-commutes with the operator
D_{\Phi^{pre}}
. Thus, on a closed manifold we deduce that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{ker}}_{L^{2}}(D_{\Phi^{pre}})={\mathrm{ker}}_{L^{2}}(D_{\Phi^{pre}}^{op})\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt{\mathrm{im}}_{L^{2}}(D_{\Phi^{pre}})={\mathrm{im}}_{L^{2}}(D_{\Phi^{pre}}^{op}).

The idea of Joyce was to use singular perturbation theory to obtain a torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure. We will briefly summarize these ideas and state the Joyce-existence result for torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures.

Let
\Phi^{pre}
be a
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure on an eight manifold
X
with small torsion. The condition of a
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure in a small neighbourhood of
{\Phi^{pre}}
to be torsion-free is given by

\displaystyle 0={\mathrm{d}}\Theta({\Phi^{pre}}+\eta)=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\Phi^{pre}+{\mathrm{d}}\pi_{\tau,\Phi^{pre}}(\eta)+{\mathrm{d}}Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta)

Instead of constructing a
{\mathrm{Diff}}_{0}(X)
-orbit full of solutions, it is more convenient to construct a solution in
\mathfrak{Spin}(7)(X)
. Thus, we need to impose the gauge fixing condition of the form

\displaystyle\pi_{8,\Phi^{pre}}{\mathrm{d}}\pi_{\tau,\Phi^{pre}}(\eta)=0

which defines a gauge slice for the
{\mathrm{Diff}}_{0}(X)
-action on
\Gamma(X,Cay_{+}(X))

[
Joy96a
, Thm. D]
. In the case
\hat{A}(X)=1
, Joyce
[
Joy96a
, p.32]
argued that this gauge slice can be replaced by

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}{\Phi^{pre}}+{\mathrm{d}}\pi_{\tau,{\Phi^{pre}}}(\eta)+{\mathrm{d}}Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta)

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\pi_{1\oplus 7,\Phi^{pre}}\eta.

Now,
\pi_{1\oplus 7,\Phi^{pre}}\eta=0
implies
\eta=-*_{\Phi^{pre}}\eta
and hence, by taking the
*_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}
of the first equation we obtain

(6)

\displaystyle-{\mathrm{d}}\eta=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\Phi^{pre}+{\mathrm{d}}Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta)

(7)

\displaystyle-{\mathrm{d}}^{*_{\Phi^{pre}}}\eta=

\displaystyle*_{\Phi^{pre}}{\mathrm{d}}\Phi^{pre}+*_{\Phi^{pre}}{\mathrm{d}}Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta).

Using these operators, we are able to express (
6
) and (
7
) as

(8)

\displaystyle-D_{\Phi^{pre}}\eta=D_{\Phi^{pre}}\pi_{-,\Phi^{pre}}\left\{Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta)\right\}+D^{op}_{\Phi^{pre}}\left(\Phi^{pre}+\pi_{+,\Phi^{pre}}\left\{Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta)\right\}\right).

Let
R_{\Phi^{pre}}
denote a right-inverse of
D_{\Phi^{pre}}
and let
R_{\Phi^{pre}}\gamma=\eta
. Then
\eta
is a solution to the above if
\gamma
is a solution to the fixpoint problem

(9)

\displaystyle\gamma=

\displaystyle-D_{\Phi^{pre}}\pi_{-,\Phi^{pre}}\left\{Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(R_{\Phi^{pre}}\gamma)\right\}-D^{op}_{\Phi^{pre}}\left(\Phi^{pre}+\pi_{+,\Phi^{pre}}\left\{Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(R_{\Phi^{pre}}\gamma)\right\}\right).

Alternatively, we can restrict ourselves to
\eta\in{\mathrm{ker}}(D_{\Phi^{pre}})^{\perp}={\mathrm{im}}(R_{\Phi^{pre}})
and solve for

\displaystyle\eta=-(D_{\Phi^{pre}}|_{{\mathrm{ker}}(D_{\Phi^{pre}})^{\perp}})^{-1}\left(D_{\Phi^{pre}}\pi_{-,\Phi^{pre}}\left\{Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta)\right\}+D^{op}_{\Phi^{pre}}\left(\Phi^{pre}+\pi_{+,\Phi^{pre}}\left\{Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta)\right\}\right)\right)

as
D_{\Phi^{pre}}\pi_{-,\Phi^{pre}}\left\{Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta)\right\}+D^{op}_{\Phi^{pre}}\left(\Phi^{pre}+\pi_{+,\Phi^{pre}}\left\{Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta)\right\}\right)\in{\mathrm{ker}}(D_{\Phi^{pre}})^{\perp}
.

Remark 2
.

Let
X
be simply connected and
\hat{A}(X)=1
. The moduli space is of dimension
{\mathrm{dim}}(\mathfrak{Spin}(7)[X])=\hat{A}(X)+b^{1}(X)+b^{4}_{-}(X)=1+b^{4}_{-}(X)
. Given a solution
\Phi=\Theta(\Phi^{pre}+\eta)
of (
8
), a local neighbourhood of
[\Phi]
is modelled by a small neighbourhood of the zero in
\mathbb{R}\Phi\oplus{\mathrm{ker}}(D_{\Phi^{pre}})
.

In order to show that (
8
) has a solution, we need to show that the right-hand side of (
9
) defines a contractive mapping. The following quadratic estimate on
Q_{\Phi}
was used by Joyce to prove the existence of a solution.

Lemma 2.3
.

[
Joy96a
, Lem. 5.1.1]

Let
X
be an oriented eight manifold,
\Phi^{pre}
a
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure and
g_{\Phi^{pre}}
the corresponding Riemannian metric. Let
\eta\in\Omega^{4}(X)
and
|\eta|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}<\rho
such that
\Phi^{pre}+\phi\in\Gamma(X,Tub_{\rho}(Cay_{+}(X)))
and

(10)

\displaystyle\Theta(\Phi^{pre}+\eta)=\Phi^{pre}+\pi_{\tau,\Phi^{pre}}(\eta)+Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta)

where
Q_{\Phi^{pre}}\colon B_{\rho,g_{\Phi^{pre}}}(\wedge^{4}T^{*}X)\rightarrow\wedge^{4}T^{*}X
is a smooth map satisfying
Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(0)=0
. For all
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\Omega^{4}(X)
with
|\eta|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}},|\eta^{\prime}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}<\rho

(11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}|Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta_{1})-Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta_{2})|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}\lesssim&\hskip 14.22636pt|\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}(|\eta_{1}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}+|\eta_{2}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}})\end{split}

and

(12)

\displaystyle|\nabla^{pre}Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta_{1})-\nabla^{pre}Q_{\Phi^{pre}}(\eta_{2})|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636ptc_{0}\cdot|\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}

\displaystyle+c_{1}\cdot|\nabla^{pre}(\eta_{1}-\eta_{2})|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}

where

\displaystyle c_{0}(\Phi^{pre},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt|{\mathrm{d}}\Phi^{pre}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}(|\eta_{1}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}+|\eta_{2}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}})+|\nabla^{pre}\eta_{1}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}+|\nabla^{pre}\eta_{2}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}

\displaystyle+|\nabla^{pre}\eta_{1}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}|\eta_{2}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}+|\eta_{1}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}|\nabla^{pre}\eta_{2}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}

\displaystyle c_{1}(\Phi^{pre},\eta_{1},\eta_{2})=

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt|\eta_{1}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}+|\eta_{2}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}+|\eta_{2}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}|\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}|_{g_{\Phi^{pre}}}.

The metric and the connection are the ones induced by
{\Phi^{pre}}
.

In his seminal work
[
Joy96a
]
, Joyce used this estimate to prove that under certain conditions (
8
) has a smooth solution, i.e. that in some cases
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures with small torsion can be deformed into torsion-free ones.

Theorem 2.3
.

[
Joy96a
, Thm. A]

Let
X
be a compact eight-dimensional manifold and
\Phi^{pre}\in\mathcal{C}ay(X)
. Assume that there exists a smooth four form
\eta
such that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}(\Phi^{pre}+\eta)=0

and that the following four conditions hold

•

\left|\left|\eta\right|\right|_{L^{2}}\lesssim t^{9/2}

•

\left|\left|\eta\right|\right|_{L^{10}}\lesssim t

•

If
{\chi}\in W^{1,10}\Omega^{4}_{-}(X)
then

\displaystyle\left|\left|\nabla{\chi}\right|\right|_{L^{10}}\lesssim\left|\left|{\mathrm{d}}{\chi}\right|\right|_{L^{10}}+t^{-21/5}\cdot\left|\left|{\chi}\right|\right|_{L^{2}}

•

If
{\chi}\in W^{1,10}\Omega^{4}_{-}(X)
then
{\chi}\in\Omega^{0;4}_{-}(X)
and

\displaystyle\left|\left|{\chi}\right|\right|_{C^{0}}\lesssim t^{1/5}\cdot\left|\left|\nabla{\chi}\right|\right|_{L^{10}}+t^{-4}\cdot\left|\left|{\chi}\right|\right|_{L^{2}}

Then there exists a smooth torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure
\Phi
satisfying
\left|\left|\Phi^{pre}-\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}}\lesssim t^{1/2}
.

Notation
.

In the above Theorem,
W^{1,10}\Omega^{4}_{-}(X)
denotes the Sobolev space of anti-self dual four forms that are weakly differentiable and the Sobolev norm is taken with respect to
L^{10}
, while
\Omega^{0;4}_{-}(X)
denotes the space of
C^{0}
anti-self-dual forms.

3.
Hodge Theory on Tame Gromov-Hausdorff-Resolutions of Riemannian Orbifolds

As recalled in (
8
), the condition of deforming a nearly torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure into a torsion-free one as a nonlinear fixed-point problem involving a right-inverse of the Hodge–de Rham operator. Solving this deformation problem requires uniform analytic control of the Hodge theory across families of resolutions.

Here we develop such a framework in the general setting of tame smooth Gromov–Hausdorff-resolutions of Riemannian orbifolds. Building on the analytic results of
[
Maj25b
]
, we establish Hodge theory for these resolutions, showing how harmonic forms and cohomology behave under the resolution process. The key point is to construct and compare harmonic forms on the resolution with those on the singular model, in a way that is compatible with gluing techniques.

The section is structured as follows. In Section
3.1
we introduce smooth and tame Gromov–Hausdorff-resolutions, formalising the notion of resolving orbifolds by families of smooth manifolds converging in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. Section
3.2
computes the cohomology of resolved orbifolds and establishes comparison results with the orbifold cohomology. Section
3.3
develops a linear gluing theory for the Hodge–de Rham operator, relating harmonic representatives on the resolution to model operators on the conical fibrations.

These results form the analytic backbone of the paper. In Section
4
they are applied to determine the resolution data required for
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold singularities, and in Section
5
they are used to solve the deformation problem and prove the existence of torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold resolutions.

3.1.
Smooth Gromov-Hausdorff-Resolutions

In the following subsection we will define smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolutions of a Riemannian orbifold and describe how these are constructed using geometric resolution data.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(X,g)
be a Riemannian orbifold. A
smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolution
of
(X,g)
is given by a smooth family of (singular) Riemannian orbifolds
(X_{t},g_{t})
and morphisms

\displaystyle\rho_{t}\colon X_{t}\dashrightarrow X

such that
\rho_{t}
restricts to an orbifold diffeomorphism
(\rho_{t})^{-1}(X^{reg})\cong X^{reg}
, the exceptional set
\Upsilon_{t}=(\rho_{t})^{-1}(X^{sing})
is of codimension
>0
and

\displaystyle(X_{t},g_{t})\xrightarrow[GH]{t\to 0}(X,g)\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt(X_{t}\backslash\Upsilon_{t},g_{t})\xrightarrow[C^{\infty}]{t\to 0}(X^{reg},g).

In particular,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{vol}}_{g_{t}}(\Upsilon_{t})\xrightarrow{t\to 0}0\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt(\rho_{t})_{*}g_{t}\xrightarrow[C^{\infty}]{t\to 0}g.

A complete smooth Gromov-Hausdorff-resolution of
(X,g)
is given by a smooth family of Riemannian manifolds
(X_{t},g_{t})
resolving
(X,g)
at
X^{sing}
. Smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolutions of Riemannian orbifolds form a category
\widetilde{\textbf{GHRes}}(X,g)
. A smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolution
(X_{t},g_{t},\rho_{t})
is a
(0,\epsilon)
-family of Riemannian orbifold resolutions. A morphism
(\rho_{t},X_{t},g_{t})\rightsquigarrow(\rho^{\prime}_{t},X^{\prime}_{t},g^{\prime}_{t})
of Riemannian orbifold resolutions is given by a
(0,\min\{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\})
-family of commuting diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(X_{t},g_{t})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(X^{\prime}_{t},g^{\prime}_{t})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(X,g)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{\prime}_{t}}

Two resolutions
(\rho_{t},X_{t},g_{t})
and
(\rho^{\prime}_{t},X_{t},g^{\prime}_{t})
are called equivalent, if the morphisms
\phi_{t}
are isometries of Riemannian orbifolds. The category of smooth Gromov-Hausdorff-resolutions of Riemannian is the quotient category

\displaystyle\textbf{GHRes}(X,g)=\widetilde{\textbf{GHRes}}(X,g)/\sim.

\lozenge

Remark 3
.

With this definition, there is no need to repeatedly shrink the parameter range
(0,T)
at each step. Some arguments may only apply to a smaller subfamily
(0,T^{\prime})
, but this is irrelevant for our purposes. When discussing resolutions of Riemannian orbifolds, we are typically not concerned with the maximal interval of existence, but rather with the existence of a resolution at all. The above “germ-like” definition of the resolution category allows us to avoid unnecessary technicalities. Starting from a resolution
(X_{t},g_{t})\in\widetilde{\textbf{GHRes}}(X,g)
, certain analytic steps may require restricting the family to
(0,\epsilon^{\prime})\subset(0,\epsilon)
; however, in the category
\textbf{GHRes}(X,g)
, these are identified as the same object, and no distinction is needed.

3.1.1.
Riemannian Orbifolds as Conically Fibred Singular Spaces

In this section, we recall the notion of conically fibred (CF) spaces and conically fibred singular (CFS) spaces as a systematic way to describe the local geometry of Riemannian orbifolds and more general stratified spaces. This viewpoint refines the classical description of quasi-conical neighbourhoods near singular strata by emphasising the fibration structure of each stratum, which plays a central role in resolution constructions.

Let in the following
\vartheta\colon(Y,g_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})
be a Riemannian fibration
7
7

7

Here we allow the Riemannian structure on the fibres to be singular.
of dimension
m-1+s=n-1
(here
S
is an
s
-dimensional Riemannian manifold) with compact fibres. The Riemannian structure on
Y
induces an Ehresmann connection on the fibration, i.e.
TY\cong H_{Y}\oplus VY
.

Definition 3.2
.

A
conical fibration (CF)
over a link-fibration
(\vartheta\colon(Y,g_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}))
is given by the (singular) warped product

\displaystyle\left(\nu_{0}\colon(CF_{S}(Y),g_{{\mathrm{CF}}}\right)\rightarrow(S,g_{S}))\coloneqq\left((\vartheta\circ{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}\colon\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times Y,{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+g^{2,0}_{Y}+r^{2}g^{0,2}_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})\right)

Usually, we will write
g_{{\mathrm{CF}}}=\vartheta^{*}g_{S}+g_{{\mathrm{CF}};V}
where
g_{{\mathrm{CF}};V}
denotes the fibrewise conical structure. The connection
H_{Y}
induces the connection
H_{{\mathrm{CF}}}
on
CF_{S}(Y)
via

\displaystyle({\mathrm{id}}\oplus A_{H_{Y}})\colon TCF_{S}(Y)\cong\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus TY\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus VY.

A singular Riemannian manifold
(X_{{\mathrm{CFS}}},g_{{\mathrm{CFS}}})
is called
conically fibred singular (CFS)
of rate
\sigma
if there exists a compact subset
K_{R}
and a diffeomorphism
\rho:X_{{\mathrm{CFS}}}\backslash K_{R}\rightarrow[0,R)\times Y
such that

\displaystyle g_{{\mathrm{CFS}}}=g_{{\mathrm{CFS}};0}+g_{{\mathrm{CFS}};hot}\hskip 28.45274pt|g_{{\mathrm{CFS}};hot}|_{g_{{\mathrm{CF}}}}=\mathcal{O}(r^{\sigma})

and

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{{\mathrm{CF}}}})^{k}\left(\rho_{*}g_{{\mathrm{CFS}};0}-g_{{\mathrm{CF}}}\right)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{\sigma})

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{{\mathrm{CF}}}}_{V})^{k}\left(\rho_{*}g_{{\mathrm{CFS}};0}-g_{{\mathrm{CF}}}\right)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{\sigma-k}).

By pulling back the projection
\nu_{0}
as well as the connection
H_{{\mathrm{CF}}}
we obtain a fibre bundle

\displaystyle\nu_{0}:X_{{\mathrm{CFS}}}\backslash K_{R}\rightarrow S\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274ptT(X_{CFS}\backslash K_{R})\cong H_{{\mathrm{CFS}}}\oplus V(X_{{\mathrm{CFS}}}\backslash K_{R}).

\lozenge

Remark 4
.

Notice that the geometry of the conical fibration is the one of a warped product

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times Y,{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+g_{Y;r})

of the real half-line and a collapsing fibration

\displaystyle(Y,g_{Y;r}=g^{2,0}_{Y}+r^{2}g^{0,2}_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}).

Let
i\colon(S,g_{S})\hookrightarrow(X,g)
be a connected singular stratum of codimension
m
whose isotropy group is
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{O}}(m)
. We define the normal bundle of
S
via

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TS}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces NS.}

We will think of
NS
as a vector bundle
\nu\colon NS\rightarrow S
with a fibrewise
\Gamma
-action. We define the normal cone bundle to be the conically fibred space

\displaystyle\nu_{0}\colon N_{0}\coloneqq NS/\Gamma\rightarrow S.

Let further
Fr(X/S,g)
be the natural
{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{O}}(n)}(\Gamma)
-reduction of the
{\mathrm{O}}(n)
frame bundle
Fr_{{\mathrm{O}}(n)}(X)
of
(X,g)
restricted to
S
. Define the associated (orbifold) bundle

\displaystyle Y\coloneqq Fr(X/S,g)\times_{{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{O}}(n)}(\Gamma)}\mathbb{S}^{m-1}/\Gamma\xrightarrow{\vartheta}S.

Whenever
(S,g_{S})
is of type A, the bundle
Y
is smooth. The Riemannian orbifold structure
g
induces a splitting

\displaystyle TN_{0}\cong H_{0}\oplus VN_{0}\cong\nu^{*}_{0}TS\oplus\nu^{*}_{0}NS

and a Riemannian orbifold structure on the normal cone bundle
N_{0}
such that

\displaystyle g_{0}=\nu^{*}i^{*}g=\nu^{*}_{0}g_{S}+g_{0;V}\in\Gamma\left(N_{0},{\mathrm{Sym}}^{2}H_{0}^{*}\oplus{\mathrm{Sym}}^{2}V^{*}N_{0}\right).

Equivalently, we can think of a Riemannian orbifold structure on
(N_{0},g_{0})
as a real Hermitian Lie algebroid structure on
(\prescript{ie}{}{}T[N_{0}\colon S],g_{0})
.

Let
\epsilon>0
and let

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Tub}}_{5\epsilon}(S)\coloneqq(Y\times(0,5\epsilon))\subset N_{0}

and

\displaystyle{\mathrm{exp}}_{g}\colon{\mathrm{Tub}}_{5\epsilon}(S)\rightarrow U_{5\epsilon}\subset X

be a tubular neighbourhood of width
5\epsilon
, induced by the Riemannian structure.
8
8

8

Later we will assume that
\epsilon\sim t^{\lambda}
for
0\leq\lambda<1
.
Let
r\colon N_{0}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
denote the radius function on the normal cone bundle. Then there exists an asymptotic expansion

\displaystyle{\mathrm{exp}}_{g}^{*}g=g_{0}+g_{hot}

and the higher order terms satisfy

\displaystyle|g_{hot}|_{g_{0}}=\mathcal{O}(r).

This structure is referred to as in the literature (see
[
Gra03
, Chap. 2]
) as a Fermi atlas with respect to
S
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
S\subset X
be a singular stratum of a Riemannian orbifold
(X,g)
. Its normal cone bundle
\nu_{0}:(N_{0},g_{0})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})
is a Riemannian CF-space. A tubular neighbourhood of the singular stratum endows
(X,g)
with the structure of a Riemannian CFS-space of rate
1
.

3.1.2.
Local Models of Resolutions

In the following we will axiomatises the local model of resolutions of CFS spaces at the working example of Riemannian orbifolds. We will introduce the concept of asymptotically conical fibrations (ACF).

Definition 3.3
.

A Riemannian fibre bundle
\tilde{p}:(X_{{\mathrm{ACF}}},g_{{\mathrm{ACF}}})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})
is an
asymptotic conical fibration (ACF)
over a fibration
(q:(Y,g_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}))
of rate
\gamma
, if there exists compact subset
K_{R}
and a fibre bundle diffeomorphism
\rho:X_{{\mathrm{ACF}}}\backslash K_{R}\rightarrow CF_{S}(E)\backslash B_{R}(E)
such that

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{{\mathrm{CF}}}})^{k}\left(\rho_{*}g_{{\mathrm{ACF}}}-g_{{\mathrm{CF}}}\right)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{\gamma})

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{{\mathrm{CF}}}}_{V})^{k}\left(\rho_{*}g_{{\mathrm{ACF}};V}-g_{{\mathrm{CF}};V}\right)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{\gamma-k}).

We will further demand that the ACF metric is of the form
g_{{\mathrm{ACF}}}=\tilde{p}^{*}g_{S}+g_{{\mathrm{ACF}};V}
and hence, we equip
p:(X_{{\mathrm{ACF}}},g_{{\mathrm{ACF}}})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})
with a connection
H_{{\mathrm{ACF}}}
.

\lozenge

Generalising the notion of these noncompact geometries leads to the notion of QACF spaces. These geometries include all local models of orbifold resolutions.

Definition 3.4
.

A Riemannian fibre bundle
\tilde{\nu}\colon(X,g_{QACF})\rightarrow S
is
quasi asymptotic to a conical fibration (QACF)
over a fibration
(\overline{\nu}\colon Y\rightarrow S,g_{0;Y})
of rate
\gamma
, if there exists subset
S_{R}
and a fibre bundle diffeomorphism
\rho_{QACF}\colon X\backslash S_{R}\rightarrow QCF_{S}(Y)
such that

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{QCF}})^{k}\left(\rho_{*_{QACF}}g-g_{QCF}\right)=\mathcal{O}(r^{\gamma}).

where

\displaystyle g_{QCF}\coloneqq g^{2,0}_{Y}+{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+r^{2}(g_{1/r;Y})^{0,2}

and where

\displaystyle(\widetilde{Y},g^{0,2}_{1/r;Y})\xrightarrow[GH]{r\to\infty}(Y,g^{0,2}_{Y})

is a family of (singular) Riemannian metrics converging in the Gromov-Hausdorff sense to the (singular)
(Y,g^{0,2}_{0,Y})
.

\lozenge

A special class of spaces that are (Q)ACF spaces are the so called (Quasi) Asymptotically Locally Euclidean ((Q)ALE) spaces. They are formed by the subclass of the former that fibre over the point
S=\{pt.\}
and were introduced by Joyce in
[
Joy00
, Chap. 8 and 9]
. Moreover, given a
G
-structure on these noncompact spaces, we will specify their asymptotic behaviour compared to a flat model structure.

Let in the following
(V,\Phi_{V})
be a
m
-dimensional Euclidean space and
\Gamma\subset G\subset{\mathrm{SO}}(V,g_{V})
a finite subgroup, with a flat, translational invariant
G
-structure
\Phi_{V}\in\Gamma(V,Str_{G}(V))^{V}
.

Definition 3.5
.

Assume that
\Gamma
acts freely on
V\backslash\{0\}
. An orbifold
(M,\Phi)
with a torsion-free
G
-structure
\Phi\in\Gamma(M,Str_{G}(M))
is called an
asymptotically locally Euclidean (ALE)

G
-structure of rate
\gamma
, if there exists a compact subset
B_{R}\subset M
and a diffeomorphisms

\displaystyle\rho:M\backslash B_{R}\rightarrow(V\backslash B_{R}(0))/\Gamma

and the
G
-structure satisfies

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{V}})^{k}\left(\rho_{*}\Phi-\Phi_{V}\right)=\mathcal{O}(r^{\gamma-k}),

where
r:V/\Gamma\rightarrow[0,\infty)
denotes the radius function.

\lozenge

Remark 5
.

By a classical result of Bando, Kasue and Nakajima
[
BKN89
]
Euclidean volume growth and
L^{2}
-integrable curvature implies that a non-compact, Ricci-flat manifold
(M,g)
is ALE of rate
\gamma=1-m
. If further
m=4
or
g
Kähler, then
\gamma=-m
.

Let
\Gamma\subset G\subset{\mathrm{SO}}(V,g_{V})
be a finite subgroup acting on
V
in a possible non-free way. Previously we assumed that the stratum of points with nontrivial stabiliser was only the origin; now we allow nontrivial linear subspaces to be stabilised by nontrivial subgroups of
\Gamma
.

We define

\displaystyle Fix(\Gamma_{i})=

\displaystyle\{v\in V|{\mathrm{Stab}}(v)=\Gamma_{i}\subset\Gamma\}

\displaystyle C(T)=

\displaystyle\{g\in\Gamma|g.t=t,\forall t\in T\}

\displaystyle N(T)=

\displaystyle\{g\in\Gamma|g.T=T\}

and denote by
\mathcal{L}=\{T_{i}=Fix(\Gamma_{i})|\Gamma_{i}\subset\Gamma\}
the set of all fixpoint vector spaces and let
I
be an indexing set for
\mathcal{L}
. Set
T_{0}=Fix(1)=V
and
T_{\infty}=Fix(\Gamma)=0
and define a partial ordering
i\succeq j
if
T_{i}\subset T_{j}
. If
V=T_{i}\oplus T_{i}
, then
T_{i}=Fix(C(T_{i}))
and
C(T_{i})
act on
V/C(T_{i})\cong T_{i}\times S_{i}/C(V_{i})
. In particular,
W/N(T_{i})=(T_{i}\times S_{i}/C(T_{i}))/(N(T_{i})/C(T_{i}))
.

Let
T_{i},T_{j}\in\mathcal{L}
. Then

\displaystyle C(T_{i})C(T_{j})=C(T_{i}\cap T_{j})

and consequently, the set
\mathcal{L}
is closed under intersections. Moreover, the group
\Gamma
acts on the indexing set
I
, by
T_{g.i}=g.T_{i}
.

We can identify the singular set of
V/\Gamma
with the set

\displaystyle Sing(V/\Gamma)=\bigcup_{i\in I\backslash\{0\}}T_{i}/\Gamma.

For a generic point
[v]\in Sing(V/\Gamma)
the singularity of
V/\Gamma
at
[v]
is modelled on
T_{i}\times S_{i}/C(T_{i})
.

Let
Sing(V/\Gamma)
be the singular set of
V/\Gamma
. The singular set is given by the union

\displaystyle Sing(V/\Gamma)=\bigcup_{i\in I}T_{i}/\Gamma.

Define the distance functions for
0\neq i\in I

\displaystyle d_{i}:V/\Gamma\rightarrow[0,\infty),\hskip 28.45274ptv\mapsto{\mathrm{dist}}_{g_{V}}(v,T_{i}).

Notice that
d_{\infty}=r
is the standard radius function on
V/\Gamma
and that
d_{j}\leq d_{i}
for
i\succeq i
.

Definition 3.6
.

A noncompact, connected
m
-dimensional, orbifold
(M,\Phi)
with a torsion-free
G
-structure
\Phi\in\Gamma(M,Str_{G}(M))
is called
quasi asymptotically locally Euclidean (QALE)
G
-structure
of rate
\gamma
, if there exists a asymptotically cylindrical suborbifold
9
9

9

Here asymptotic cylindrical is meant in the topological sense.

S_{R}
and a diffeomorphism

\displaystyle\rho:M\backslash S_{R}\rightarrow(V/\Gamma)\backslash{\mathrm{Tub}}_{R}(Sing(V/\Gamma))

such that

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{V}})^{k}(\rho_{*}\Phi-\Phi_{V})=\sum_{0\neq i\in I}\mathcal{O}(d_{i}^{\gamma-k})

for all
k\geq 0
.

\lozenge

Definition 3.7
.

Let
(M,\Phi)
be a QALE
G
-structure. A set of QALE distance functions
\{\delta_{i}\}_{0\neq i\in I}
on
M
are functions
\delta_{i}:M\rightarrow[1,\infty)
satisfying

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{V}})^{k}(\rho_{*}\delta_{i}-d_{i})=\mathcal{O}(d_{i}^{1+\gamma-k})

for all
k\geq 0
.

\lozenge

3.1.3.
Interpolating and
k
-Tame Smooth Gromov-Hausdorff Resolutions

In this section we develop a systematic framework for analysing smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolutions of Riemannian orbifolds. The guiding principle is that such resolutions should be modelled, at small scales near the singular locus, by asymptotically conical fibrations (ACF) glued into the normal cone of the singular stratum. This motivates the notion of interpolating resolutions and serve as reference models for more general resolutions. To quantify how closely an arbitrary smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolution approximates a interpolating one, we introduce the notion of
k
-tameness, measured by
C^{k}_{loc}
-convergence of the associated families of metrics under suitable identifications. This formalism provides a convenient language for comparing analytic data across families, and will be the main tool for studying geometric operators, such as Dirac operators, in later sections.

The group
(\mathbb{R}_{>0},\cdot)
acts on the normal cone bundle
\nu_{0}\colon N_{0}\rightarrow S
via the dilatation of the fibres

\displaystyle\delta_{t}\colon N_{0}\rightarrow N_{0},\,[n_{s}]\mapsto[t\cdot n_{s}].

Notation
.

Given a tensor
\eta
on
N_{0}
, we will denote by

\displaystyle\eta^{t}\coloneqq\delta^{*}_{t}\eta

the
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-family induced by the action.

In particular, the CF-normal cone structure satisfies

\displaystyle g^{t}_{0}=g_{0;H}+t^{2}\cdot g_{0;V}.

Let us assume that there exists a family of ACF spaces of rate
\gamma
, forming a smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolution

\displaystyle(N_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta},g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})\in\textbf{GHRes}(N_{0},g_{0}),

such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N_{\zeta},g^{t}_{\zeta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N_{t^{2}\zeta},g_{t^{2}\zeta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N_{0},g^{t}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N_{0},g_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\zeta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{t}}

and the Riemannian structure decomposes into

\displaystyle\delta_{t}^{*}g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}=g_{\zeta;H}+t^{2}\cdot g_{\zeta;V}.

Using the splitting
H_{\zeta}\oplus VN_{\zeta}
we define the connection
\nabla^{\oplus_{\zeta}}
and its torsion tensor
T_{\oplus_{\zeta}}
. This connection is
t
-independent and we identify

\displaystyle\nabla^{g^{t}_{\zeta}}=\nabla^{\oplus_{\zeta}}+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\Lambda^{t}_{\zeta}}(T_{\oplus_{\zeta}}).

Following
[
BGV92
, (10.3)]
the limit

\displaystyle\nabla^{g^{0}_{\zeta}}=\nabla^{\oplus_{\zeta}}+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\Lambda}^{0}(T_{\oplus_{\zeta}})

is well-defined.

Definition 3.8
.

We define the
difference torsion

\displaystyle\Omega_{\zeta}=T_{\oplus_{\zeta}}-\rho_{\zeta}^{*}T_{\oplus_{0}}.

and an adapted metric connection

(13)

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}^{g^{t}_{\zeta}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\Lambda}^{t}_{\zeta}(\Omega_{\zeta}).

\lozenge

Remark 6
.

As
g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}
is ACF with respect to
g_{0}
,

\displaystyle\nabla^{k}\Omega_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}=\mathcal{O}(r^{\gamma})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt(\nabla^{0,1})^{k}\Omega_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}=\mathcal{O}(r^{\gamma-k}).

Remark 7
.

By abuse of notation we will denote the lift of the dilatation action by
\delta_{t}
.

Notation
.

Let
r\colon N_{\zeta}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
be the radius function on
N_{\zeta}
defined by
r=r\circ\rho_{\zeta}
. We will denote by

\displaystyle B_{R}(N_{\zeta})\coloneqq r^{-1}(0,R)\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274ptA_{(R_{1},R_{2})}(S)\coloneqq{\mathrm{Tub}}_{R_{2}}(S)\backslash{\mathrm{Tub}}_{R_{1}}(S).

Definition 3.9
.

We define the
gluing morphism

\Gamma_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\coloneqq{\mathrm{exp}}_{g}\circ\rho_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}
and denote the family of spaces

\displaystyle X_{\zeta;t}\coloneqq(B_{5\epsilon}(N_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})\sqcup X\backslash S)/_{\sim_{\Gamma_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}}

Furthermore, these spaces are equipped with a resolution morphism

\displaystyle\rho_{\zeta;t}\colon X_{\zeta;t}\dashrightarrow X.

\lozenge

Remark 8
.

The dilation map

\displaystyle\delta_{t}\colon(N_{\zeta},g^{t}_{\zeta})\rightarrow(N_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta},g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})

identifies the resolution data of the normal cone bundle
(N_{0},g_{0})
with the fixed fibration
\nu_{\zeta}\colon N_{\zeta}\to S
, equipped with a family of metrics
g^{t}_{\zeta}
. This allows us to define a family of spaces by gluing:

\displaystyle\rho^{t}_{\zeta}\colon X^{t}_{\zeta}\coloneqq\left(B_{5t^{-1}\epsilon}(N_{\zeta})\sqcup X\setminus S\right)/\sim_{\Gamma^{t}_{\zeta}}\dashrightarrow X,

where the gluing map is
\Gamma^{t}_{\zeta}\coloneqq\delta_{t}^{*}\Gamma_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}
. The spaces
X^{t}_{\zeta}
and
X_{\zeta;t}
are canonically diffeomorphic via the rescaling:

\displaystyle\delta_{t}\colon X^{t}_{\zeta}\cong X_{\zeta;t}\colon\delta_{t^{-1}}.

We emphasize that
X^{t}_{\zeta}
and
X_{\zeta;t}
describe the same space, but the notation reflects different perspectives: we use the superscript
X^{t}_{\zeta}
to indicate the viewpoint relative to
N_{\zeta}
, and the subscript
X_{\zeta;t}
when viewing the space relative to
X
. This distinction is particularly useful since the gluing map
\Gamma^{t}_{\zeta}
incorporates the rescaling map
\delta_{t}
, and it is often convenient to switch between the two pictures depending on the geometric or analytic context.

Let
{\chi}\colon[0,1]\rightarrow[0,1]
be a smooth positive, function such that
{\mathrm{supp}}({\chi})\subset(0,1]
and
{\mathrm{supp}}(1-{\chi})\subset[0,1)
fixed throughout this work. Define the smooth functions

\displaystyle{\chi_{i}}(x)\coloneqq

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}0&x\in B_{(i-1)\epsilon}(N_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})\\
{\chi}((i-1)\epsilon^{-1}r-i+1)&x\in B_{i\epsilon}(N_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})\backslash B_{(i-1)\epsilon}(N_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})\\
1&\text{else}\end{array}\right.

and set
\delta^{*}_{t}\chi_{i}=\chi^{t}_{i}
.

Notation
.

Given two sections
\Psi
and
\Phi
of mutually vector bundles on
N_{\zeta}
and
X
that “glue” together by a lift
\hat{\Gamma}_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}
of
\Gamma_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}
, we define a new section on
X_{t}
by

\displaystyle\Psi\cup^{t}\Phi\coloneqq

\displaystyle(1-\chi^{t}_{3})\Psi+\chi^{t}_{3}(\hat{\Gamma}^{t}_{\zeta})^{*}(\Gamma^{t}_{\zeta})_{*}\Phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\chi_{3})(\hat{\Gamma}^{t^{-1}}_{\zeta})^{*}(\Gamma^{t^{-1}}_{\zeta})_{*}\Psi+\chi_{3}\Phi\eqqcolon(\hat{\Gamma}^{t^{-1}}_{\zeta})^{*}(\Gamma^{t^{-1}}_{\zeta})_{*}\Psi\cup_{t}\Phi

Instead of
(\hat{\Gamma}^{t}_{\zeta})^{*}(\Gamma^{t}_{\zeta})_{*}\Phi
we will abuse the notation and write
\delta_{t}^{*}\Phi
.

Definition 3.10
.

We interpolate between the Riemannian orbifold structure on
X
and the ACF structure on
B_{5t^{-1}\epsilon}(N_{\zeta})
using the cut-off function
{\chi^{t}_{3}}
in a less naive way by

\displaystyle g^{pre;t}_{\zeta}\coloneqq g^{t}_{\zeta}\cup^{t}g_{0}+\delta^{*}_{t}g_{hot}=g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\cup_{t}g_{0}+g_{hot}\eqqcolon g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}.

\lozenge

Using this adapted connection can be seen as a leading order term in the expansion of the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{g_{\zeta;t}^{pre}}
. The following lemma makes this statement more precise.

Lemma 3.2
.

The Levi-Civita connection with respect to
g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}
satisfies

\displaystyle g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}(\nabla^{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}_{X}Y,Z)=g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}(\widehat{\nabla}^{g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}_{X}Y,Z)\cup_{t}g_{0}(\nabla^{\oplus}_{X}Y,Z)+\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{d}}\chi_{3}\wedge((\rho_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})_{*}g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-g_{0})(X,Y,Z)+\mathcal{O}(r).

Proof.

Let
X,Y,Z\in\mathfrak{X}(X_{\zeta;t})
. Then using Koszul’s formula

\displaystyle 2g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}(\nabla^{g_{\zeta;t}}_{X}Y,Z)=

\displaystyle Xg^{pre}_{\zeta;t}(Y,Z)+Yg^{pre}_{\zeta;t}(X,Z)-Zg^{pre}_{\zeta;t}(X,Y)

\displaystyle+g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}([X,Y],Z)-g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}([Y,Z],X)+g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}([Z,X],Y),

\nabla^{g}=\nabla^{\oplus}+\mathcal{O}(r)
we can deduce the first statement.
∎

Corollary 3.1
.

The family

\displaystyle\rho_{t}\colon(X_{\zeta;t},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\dashrightarrow(X,g)

defines a smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolution. We will refer to such Gromov-Hausdorff resolutions as being
interpolating
.

In the following paper, we will analyse smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolutions that are constructed through the described construction. In many cases, smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolutions of interest are “close” to such resolutions constructed form such limiting data. This naturally leads to the following definition:

Definition 3.11
.

A smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolution

\displaystyle[\rho_{t}\colon(X_{t},g_{t})\dashrightarrow(X,g)]\in\textbf{GHRes}(X,g)

is called
k
-tame
, if there exists a interpolating smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolution

\displaystyle[\rho_{\zeta;t}\colon(X_{\zeta;t},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\dashrightarrow(X,g)]\in\textbf{GHRes}(X,g)

and a family of diffeomorphisms

\displaystyle f_{t}\colon X_{t}\cong X_{\zeta;t}

such that

\displaystyle f_{t}^{*}g_{t}\xrightarrow[C^{k}_{-1;t}]{t\to 0}g_{\zeta;t}^{pre}.

Here
C^{k}_{-1;t}
is a weighted Hölder norm defined in
[
Maj25b
, Def. 9.4]
.

\lozenge

In order to study the analytic properties of families of geometric operators, such as Dirac operators, on
k
-tame smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolution
[\rho_{t}\colon(X_{t},g_{t})\dashrightarrow(X,g)]\in\textbf{GHRes}(X,g)
it suffices to study them on the interpolating smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolutions.

To avoid cumbersome booking work and improve the readability, we will restrict ourselves throughout this paper to orbifolds with singular strata of depth one and type A. However, the results presented in this paper extend
k
-tame orbifold resolutions of general strata.

Assumption 1
.

We will assume the following:

•

The Riemannian orbifold
(X,g)
contains one singular stratum of depth one and type A.

•

The orbifold resolution

\displaystyle[\rho_{t}\colon(X_{t},g_{t})\dashrightarrow(X,g)]=[\rho_{\zeta;t}\colon(X_{\zeta;t},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\dashrightarrow(X,g)].

is interpolating.

3.2.
The Cohomology of Resolved Orbifolds

In the following section we will study the topology of the resolution by calculating the cohomology groups in terms of data on the orbifold
X
as well as the ACF space
N_{\zeta}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

There exists a short exact sequence of singular cohomology groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(X,\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(X_{\zeta;t},\mathbb{R})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta},\mathbb{R})/{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(S,\mathbb{R})}

and hence,

\displaystyle b_{k}(X_{\zeta;t})=b_{k}(X)+b_{k}(N_{\zeta})-b_{k}(S).

The cohomology
{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(X)
is the cohomology of the coarse moduli space of the orbifold
X
.

Proof.

We compute the cohomology of
X_{\zeta;t}
using relative cohomology

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k-1}({\mathrm{Tub}}_{5\epsilon}(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(X,{\mathrm{Tub}}_{5\epsilon}(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}({\mathrm{Tub}}_{5t^{-1}\epsilon}(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k-1}(B_{5t^{-1}\epsilon}(N_{\zeta}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(X_{\zeta;t},B_{5t^{-1}\epsilon}(N_{\zeta}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(X_{\zeta;t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(B_{5t^{-1}\epsilon}(N_{\zeta}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k-1}(N_{\zeta})/{\mathrm{H}}^{k-1}({\mathrm{Tub}}_{5\epsilon}(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(N_{\zeta})/{\mathrm{H}}^{k}({\mathrm{Tub}}_{5\epsilon}(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(N_{\zeta})/{\mathrm{H}}^{k}({\mathrm{Tub}}_{5\epsilon}(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}

Here we used that
N_{\zeta}
retracts onto
B_{5\epsilon}(N_{\zeta})
and
{\mathrm{Tub}}_{5\epsilon}(S)
onto
S
.
∎

3.3.
Linear Gluing

In the following section, we will apply the author’s results in
[
Maj25b
]
on the uniform elliptic theory of Dirac operators on orbifold resolutions to the Hodge-de Rham operator on Riemannian orbifold resolutions. In Section
3.2
we computed the cohomology of the resolved orbifold using topological methods. By Hodge-theory the cohomology of the resolved orbifold consists of harmonic forms. The following section relates these harmonic forms to kernels of certain Dirac operators on the three gluing regions, i.e. the CFS- the CF- and the ACF-part of the orbifold resolution.

3.3.1.
The Hodge-de Rham Operator of an Orbifold Resolution

We begin discussing the set-up of
[
Maj25b
]
in the present case, by stating general results about the signature operator.

The map

\displaystyle T^{*}X_{\zeta;t}\rightarrow{\mathrm{End}}(\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X_{\zeta;t}),\xi\mapsto\left(\omega\mapsto\xi\wedge\omega-\iota_{\xi^{\sharp_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}}}\omega\right)

satisfies the Clifford property and hence, induces a faithfully representation of

\displaystyle{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}\colon{\mathrm{Cl}}(T^{*}X_{\zeta;t},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\rightarrow\mathfrak{so}(\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X_{\zeta;t},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\subset{\mathrm{End}}(T^{*}X_{\zeta;t}).

which is skew-Hermitian with respect to
g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}
. There exists an involution-isometry

\displaystyle\epsilon_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}=i^{(\bullet(\bullet-1))}*_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}\colon\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X_{\zeta;t}\rightarrow T^{*}X_{\zeta;t}

such that
\{{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}},\epsilon_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}\}=0
, and

\displaystyle\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X_{\zeta;t}=\wedge^{\bullet}_{+_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}}T^{*}X_{\zeta;t}\oplus\wedge^{\bullet}_{-_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}}T^{*}X_{\zeta;t}.

Hence, the tuple

\displaystyle(\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X_{\zeta;t},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t},\nabla^{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}},\epsilon_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}})

forms a graded Hermitian Dirac bundle. Its Dirac operator

\displaystyle D_{\zeta;t}^{pre}:={\mathrm{d}}+{\mathrm{d}}^{*^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}&D_{\zeta;t}^{pre;-}\\
D_{\zeta;t}^{pre;+}&\end{array}\right)

and
D_{\zeta;t}^{pre;+}
is the signature operator of
(X_{\zeta;t},\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t})
.

Notice that the skew-adjoint “opposite” signature operator decomposes with respect to
\epsilon_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}
into

\displaystyle D_{\zeta;t}^{op;pre}:={\mathrm{d}}-{\mathrm{d}}^{*_{\zeta;t}^{pre}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}D^{op;pre;-}_{\zeta;t}&\\
&D^{op;pre;+}_{\zeta;t}\end{array}\right).

and hence,

\displaystyle D_{\zeta;t}^{pre}\pi_{\pm;t}=\pi_{\pm;t}D_{\zeta;t}^{pre}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274ptD_{\zeta;t}^{op;pre}\pi_{\pm;t}=\pi_{\mp;t}D_{\zeta;t}^{op;pre}.

This operator squares to
-\Delta_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}
and anti-commutes with the operator
D_{\zeta;t}^{pre}
. Thus, we deduce that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{ker}}_{L^{2}}(D_{\zeta;t}^{pre})={\mathrm{ker}}_{L^{2}}(D_{\zeta;t}^{op;pre})\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt{\mathrm{im}}_{L^{2}}(D_{\zeta;t}^{pre})={\mathrm{im}}_{L^{2}}(D_{\zeta;t}^{op;pre}).

In the following we will use the resolution data to construct operators on
X
,
N_{0}
and
N_{\zeta}
, whose analytic behaviour will determine the one of
D^{pre}_{\zeta;t}
.

Using the gluing morphisms
\Gamma^{t}_{\zeta}
and
\hat{\Gamma}^{t}_{\zeta}
we define

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N_{\zeta},g^{t}_{\zeta})\sqcup(X,g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{\zeta;t},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{X}_{t},\overline{g}_{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N_{0},g_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{\Gamma^{t}_{\zeta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{\overline{\Gamma}^{t}_{\zeta}}}

the spaces
X_{\zeta;t}
and
\overline{X}_{t}
as well as the Dirac bundles

\displaystyle\pi_{t}:

\displaystyle(\wedge^{\bullet}X_{\zeta;t},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t},\nabla^{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}})\rightarrow(X_{\zeta;t},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t})

\displaystyle\overline{\pi}_{t}:

\displaystyle(\wedge^{\bullet}\overline{X}_{t},{\mathrm{cl}}_{\overline{g}_{t}},\overline{g}_{t},\nabla^{\overline{g}_{t}})\rightarrow(\overline{X}_{t},\overline{g}_{t}).

Definition 3.12
.

We will define the operators

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(X)\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(X)

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{0}

\displaystyle\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(N_{0})\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(N_{0})

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{0;V}

\displaystyle\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(N_{0})\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(N_{0})

\displaystyle\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}

\displaystyle\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta})\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta}).

We will refer to
D={\mathrm{d}}+{\mathrm{d}}^{*}
as the orbifold signature operator,
\widehat{D}_{0}={\mathrm{d}}^{1,0}+{\mathrm{d}}^{0,1}+\left({\mathrm{d}}^{1,0}+{\mathrm{d}}^{0,1}\right)^{*}
as the normal operator
10
10

10

This work is related to the normal operator
N(D)
used in
[
Maz91
]
,
D_{0,V}={\mathrm{d}}^{0,1}+\left({\mathrm{d}}^{0,1}\right)^{*}
as the vertical or indicial operator and
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}={\mathrm{d}}^{1,0}+{\mathrm{d}}^{0,1}+t^{-1}\cdot\left({\mathrm{d}}^{1,0}+{\mathrm{d}}^{0,1}\right)^{*}+t\cdot{\mathrm{cl}}(F_{H_{\zeta}}-\rho_{\zeta}^{*}F_{H_{0}})
as the resolution of the normal operator.

\lozenge

Understanding the geometric behaviour of
D_{\zeta;t}^{pre}
will essentially reduces to understand the behaviour of the orbifold Hodge-de Rham, the resolution of the normal operator and their interaction.

In order to prove the exactness of gluing we will need to construct a functional analytic realisation of the normal operator that is an isomorphism on a comeagre set of the parameters, the so called rates characterising the realisation. The meagre set on which the normal operator fails to be an isomorphism are the so called indical roots or critical rates. These rates are determined by homogeneous solutions of the
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-invariant indicial operator
I(D)=D_{0,V}
.

The analytic relation of
\widehat{D}_{0}
as an isomorphism of Banach spaces will determine Fredholm maps, realising the two operators
D
and
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}
on function spaces “asymptotic” to the ones realising the normal operator. The sum of the indices of these two realisations coincides with the index of the operator
D^{t}
and is in particular invariant under wall-crossings of the rate. This holds even though the indices of the two realisation depend on the connected component of the space of rates.

By using these fundamental insights we will further be able to construct, in certain cases, “adapted” norms and we construct a uniformly bounded right-inverse.

3.3.2.
Weighted Function Spaces on Orbifold Resolutions

Notation
.

Let
{\mathrm{word}}^{+}_{i,j}(A,B)
denote the sum of all words of length
j
in
A
and
B
counting
i
-times the letter
A
. In particular,
{\mathrm{word}}^{+}_{i,j}(A,B)={\mathrm{word}}^{+}_{j-i,j}(B,A)
.

Definition 3.13
.

We define the space
\Omega^{\bullet;k}_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}(N_{0})
to be the space of all
C^{k}
-forms
\Psi
such that

\displaystyle\left|\left|w_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}\left\{(\nabla^{\oplus})^{j}\right\}\Psi\right|\right|_{C^{0}}<\infty

where

\displaystyle w_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}\left\{(\nabla^{\oplus_{0}})^{j}\right\}:=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{j}w_{{\mathrm{CF}};(\beta-j+i)}{\mathrm{word}}^{+}_{i,j}(\nabla^{\oplus_{0};(1,0)},\nabla^{\oplus_{0};(0,1)})

and equip the spaces
\Omega^{\bullet;k}_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}(N_{0})
with the metrics

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Psi\right|\right|_{C^{k}_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k}\left|\left|w_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}\left\{(\nabla^{\oplus_{0}})^{j}\right\}\Psi\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{g_{0}}}

The space
\Omega^{\bullet}_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}(N_{0})
is defined to be the intersection of all
\Omega^{\bullet;k}_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}(N_{0})
for all
k
. Furthermore, we define the
w_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}
-weighted Hölder spaces
\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}(N_{0})
as the completions with respect to the norms

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Psi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k}\left|\left|w_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}\left\{(\nabla^{\oplus_{0}})^{j}\right\}\Psi\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{g_{0}}}+[(\nabla^{\oplus_{0}})^{j}\Psi]_{C^{0,\alpha}_{\beta-CF}}

\displaystyle[(\nabla^{\oplus_{0}})^{j}\Psi]_{C^{0,\alpha}_{\beta-CF}}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\underset{B_{g_{0}}}{{\mathrm{sup}}}\left\{\bigoplus_{i=0}^{j}w_{{\mathrm{CF}};(\beta-j+i-\alpha)}(x,y)\frac{\left|\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}_{i,j}\Psi(x)-\Pi^{x,y}\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}_{i,j}\Psi(y)\right|_{g}}{d_{g_{0}}(x,y)^{\alpha}}\right\}

where
\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}_{i,j}\coloneqq{\mathrm{word}}^{+}_{i,j}(\nabla^{\oplus_{0};(1,0)},\nabla^{\oplus_{0};(0,1)})
,
\Pi^{x,y}
denotes the parallel transport along the geodesic connecting
x
and
y
, and

\displaystyle B_{g_{0}}(U)\coloneqq\{x,y\in U|\,x\neq y,\,{\mathrm{dist}}_{g_{0}}(x,y)\leq w_{{\mathrm{CF}};(\beta-j+i-\alpha)}(x,y)\}\subset U\times U

\lozenge

Using these function space the operator
\widehat{D}_{0}
can be realised as a unbounded operator

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{0}:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{dom}}_{\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}}(\widehat{D}_{0})\subset\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}(N_{0})\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta-1}(N_{0}).

We thus define the following Banach space.

.

We will denote the domain of
\widehat{D}_{0}
by

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}(\widehat{D}_{0};{\mathrm{APS}})=\left\{\left.\Psi\in\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta}(N_{0};{\mathrm{APS}})\right|\widehat{D}_{0}\Psi\in\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{CF}};\beta-1}(N_{0})\right\}

Remark 9

In the following we will extend the notion of CF-weighted Hölder spaces to CFS- and ACF-weighted Hölder spaces. For the precise definition we refer to
[

Given the exponential tubular neighbourhood
{\mathrm{exp}}_{g_{\Phi}}\colon{\mathrm{Tub}}_{5\epsilon}(S)\hookrightarrow X

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{CFS}};\beta;\epsilon}(X)\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{CFS}};\beta;\epsilon}(X;{\mathrm{APS}})

by interpolating between the CF-weighted norms on the tubular neighbourhood and “unweighted” Hölder norms on the compact part of the
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold.

The spaces
(N_{\zeta},\Phi^{t}_{\zeta},g^{t}_{\zeta})
are ACF, i.e. asymptotic to
(N_{0},\Phi^{t}_{0},g^{t}_{0})
. By interpolating between the CF-weighted Hölder norms on the non-compact parts of

and the “unweighted” Hölder norms on the compact region, we define the ACF-weighted Hölder norms

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};\beta;t}(N_{\zeta})\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};\beta;t}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta};{\mathrm{APS}}).

By the construction, the spaces

contain forms, that fibrewise restrict to
C^{k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}

[
LMO85
]
. The vertical Dirac operator of

, i.e. the vertical Hodge-de Rham operator, can be realised as semi-Fredholm map

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{\zeta;V}\colon\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(\nu_{\zeta}^{-1}(s))\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta-1}(\nu_{\zeta}^{-1}(s)).

outside a meagre set of critical rates
\beta\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{D}_{0})
. The function space
\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};\beta}(N_{\zeta})
are well-behaved with respect to push-forwards along
\nu_{\zeta}
, and hence, we identify

with the Hölder space of section of an infinite dimensional Banach bundle of vertically AC-Hölder continuos differential forms. Using this perspective, we are able to realise the vertical Hodge-de Rham operator as a smooth homomorphism of Banach bundles over
S
. Using the AC-version of the Hodge-decomposition of differential forms

]

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{t}(S,\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{I}^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(N_{\zeta}/S)),

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{t}(S,\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(N_{\zeta}/S)),

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{t}(S,\mathcal{I}^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta-1}(N_{\zeta}/S))

and

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{t}(S,\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta-1}(N_{\zeta}/S))

of vertical differential forms that are in the coimage, kernel, image and cokernel of the vertical Hodge-de Rham. By AC elliptic regularity vertical harmonicity or coharmonicity implies vertical smoothness.

3.3.3.
Vertically Harmonic Forms

The following Lemma proves that the vertical harmonic bundles exist, the fibres are given by smooth elements that decay at least by a power of
1-m
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(N_{\zeta}/S)=\mathcal{H}_{L^{2}}(N_{\zeta}/S)

for
\beta\in(1-m,0)
.

Lemma 3.3
.

The kernel of

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{\zeta;V}(s):\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(\nu_{\zeta}^{-1}(s))\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta-1}(\nu_{\zeta}^{-1}(s))

independent of
0<\alpha<1
,
k
and
\beta\in[1-m,0)
. In particular, when
\beta<-m/2
then
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}\cong\mathcal{H}_{L^{2}}
by
[
LMO85
]
.

By the elliptic regularity of uniform elliptic asymptotically conical operator
[
Mar02
]
, the first part of the statement holds. To prove the independence in
\beta
we need the following result.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\Psi\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\nu^{-1}_{\zeta}(s))
. If
\widehat{D}_{\zeta;V}\Psi=0
and
\Psi=o(1)
then
\nabla^{k}\Psi=\mathcal{O}(r^{1-m-k})
.

Proof.

We will prove this statement similar to the proof of Proposition 5.10 in
[
Wal17
]
. Notice that
\widehat{D}_{\zeta;V}
is a Dirac operator and hence, the Kato inequality

(16)

\displaystyle|{\mathrm{d}}|\Psi||\leq\sqrt{\frac{m-1}{m}}|\nabla^{g_{\zeta;V}}\Psi|

holds on the complement of the vanishing locus of
\Psi
. By using the Weitzenböck formula

\displaystyle(\widehat{D}_{\zeta;V})^{2}=(\nabla^{g_{\zeta;V}})^{*}\nabla^{g_{\zeta;V}}+\mathcal{R}_{g_{V;\zeta}},

|F^{tw.}_{\nabla^{g_{\zeta;V}}}|=\mathcal{O}(r^{-m})
, and the identity

\displaystyle\Delta^{g_{V,\zeta}}|\Psi|^{2}+2|\nabla^{g_{\zeta;V}}\Psi|^{2}=2\left<(\nabla^{g_{\zeta;V}})^{*}\nabla^{g_{\zeta;V}}\Psi,\Psi\right>

we obtain the following estimate on the complement of the vanishing locus of
\Psi

\displaystyle\frac{2(m-1)}{m-2}\Delta^{g_{\zeta;V}}|\Psi|^{\frac{m-2}{m-1}}\leq

\displaystyle|\Psi|^{-\frac{m+1}{m}}\left(\Delta^{g_{\zeta;V}}|\Psi|^{2}+\frac{2m}{m-1}|{\mathrm{d}}|\Psi||^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\Psi|^{-\frac{m}{m-1}}\left(\Delta^{g_{\zeta;V}}|\Psi|^{2}+2|\nabla^{g_{\zeta;V}}\Psi|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2|\Psi|^{-\frac{m}{m-1}}\left<(\nabla^{g_{\zeta;V}})^{*}\nabla^{g_{\zeta;V}}\Psi,\Psi\right>

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2|\Psi|^{-\frac{m}{m-1}}\left(\left<(\widehat{D}_{\zeta;V})^{*}\widehat{D}_{\zeta;V}\Psi,\Psi\right>+\left<\mathcal{R}_{g_{V;\zeta}}\Psi,\Psi\right>\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{-m})|\Psi|^{\frac{m-2}{m-1}}

Let
f=|\Psi|^{\frac{m-2}{m-1}}
on
(V_{\Psi})^{c}=\{m\in\nu_{\zeta}^{-1}(s)|\Psi(m)\neq 0\}
. By the above

\displaystyle\Delta^{g_{V,\zeta}}f\lesssim f\cdot w_{{\mathrm{AC}};m}.

Since
f
is bounded by
[
Joy00
, Theorem 8.3.6(a)]
11
11

11

Here we used a Sobolev embedding of
W^{2,p}\hookrightarrow C^{0,\alpha}
for big enough
p>1
.
, there exists a
g=\mathcal{O}(r^{2-m})
such that

\displaystyle\Delta g=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\Delta^{g_{V,\zeta}}f)^{+}&\text{on }(V_{\Psi})^{c}\\
0&V_{\Psi}\end{array}\right.

where
(.)^{+}
denotes the positive part. As
g
is superharmonic and decays at infinity, the maximum principle implies its nonnegativity. Further, the function
f-g
is subharmonic on
(V_{\Psi})^{c}
, decays at infinity and is nonnegative on the boundary of
(V_{\Psi})^{c}
. Again, using the maximum principle
f\leq g=\mathcal{O}(r^{2-m})
and we deduce that

\displaystyle|\Psi|=\mathcal{O}(r^{1-m}).

∎

Definition 3.19
.

Define the bundle of conically
\beta
-
logarithmic solutions
of
\widehat{D}_{0;V}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{{\mathrm{C}};\beta}(\widehat{D}_{0;V})\coloneqq\left\{\Phi=\left.\sum^{n}_{i=0}{\mathrm{log}}^{i}(r)\Phi_{i}\in\Gamma_{{\mathrm{C}};\beta}(\nu_{0}^{-1}(s),\widehat{E}_{0})\right|\widehat{D}_{0;V}\Phi=0,\Phi_{i}\text{ is }\beta-\text{homogen.}\right\}

\lozenge

Remark 11
.

From now on we assume that there exists a critical rate
\mu_{\mathcal{H}}\in(-\infty,1-m]
such that the kernels

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{\zeta;V}(s):\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(\nu_{\zeta}^{-1}(s))\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta-1}(\nu_{\zeta}^{-1}(s))

are isomorphic for all
\beta\in[\mu_{\mathcal{H}},0)
. This implies that for
\beta_{2}>\beta_{1}>\mu_{\mathcal{H}}
there exists an exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{H}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta_{1}}(N_{\zeta}/S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{H}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta_{2}}(N_{\zeta}/S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{\beta_{1}<\beta<\beta_{2}}\mathcal{L}_{{\mathrm{C}},\beta}(\widehat{D}_{0;V})}}

Furthermore, there exists an isomorphism of vector bundles

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{{\mathrm{C}};\beta}(\widehat{D}_{0;V})\cong\mathcal{E}_{\beta+\frac{m-1}{2}-\delta(\widehat{E}_{0})},

where
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}
is the bundle
\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}S
twisted by the vertical
\lambda
-eigenspace bundle of the Hodge-de Rham operator on the normal cone.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
m=4
,
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
and
(N_{\zeta},g_{\zeta;V})
be fibrewise ALE.
Then
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(N_{\zeta}/S)\cong\mathcal{H}^{2}_{-}(N_{\zeta}/S)
and
\mu_{\mathcal{H}}=-4
.

Proof.

In some cases we can even further improve the Kato inequality (
16
). The only vertical harmonic forms on
N_{\zeta}
are anti-selfdual two forms. Following the results of Walter Seaman
[
Sea91
]
we improve the Kato inequality to

(17)

\displaystyle|{\mathrm{d}}|\Psi||\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}|\nabla^{g_{\zeta;V}}\Psi|.

This is achieved by a closer inspection of the Weitzenböck-curvature. Then by following the arguments of the proof of Proposition
3.2
with
|\Psi|^{2/3}
being replaced by
|\Psi|^{1/2}
we deduce the statement. Results of
[
Loc87
]
and
[
Joy00
]
ensure the existence of an isomorphism of flat vector bundles

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta}/S)\cong{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta}/S).

∎

Lemma 3.5
.

The bundles
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(N_{\zeta}/S)
and
\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{H}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(N_{\zeta}/S)
always exist and coincide with

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(N_{\zeta}/S)=\zeta^{*}\mathcal{H}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(\mathfrak{M}/\mathfrak{P})

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{H}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(N_{\zeta}/S)=\zeta^{*}\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{H}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(\mathfrak{M}/\mathfrak{P}).

Proof.

The existence of the bundles of vertically AC-harmonic and AC-coharmonic forms on
\mathfrak{M}
follows directly from the algebraic construction of
\mathfrak{M}
.
∎

.

Let
N_{\zeta}
be as above with
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Then in
[

, Thm. 3.26]

\mu_{\mathcal{K}}=-4
and

and

\displaystyle rk(\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta-1})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0,&\text{if }\beta\in(0,-2)\\
6,&\text{if }\beta\in(-2,-4)\end{array}\right.
