3.3.4.
Uniform Elliptic Theory

The following sections will be devoted on sketching the main ingredients needed to state the main theorems of the authors work in
[
Maj25b
]
.

The bundles of vertically harmonic and coharmonic bundles are finite dimensional Dirac bundles
12
12

12

For
\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{H}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}
we need
\beta\notin\mathcal{C}(\widehat{D}_{0})
.
. The operator
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}
restricts to a family of elliptic operators on
S
of Dirac type. These families
\mathfrak{D}^{t}_{\mathcal{K};\beta}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{t}_{\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K};\beta-1}
admit well-defined adiabatic limits

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{K};\beta}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\mathfrak{D}^{t}_{\mathcal{K};\beta}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K};\beta-1}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\mathfrak{D}^{t}_{\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K};\beta-1}

which will be referred to as the adiabatic residues of
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}
.

Definition 3.20
.

We will refer to the space

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Ker}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};\beta}(\widehat{D}^{0}_{\zeta})={\mathrm{ker}}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{K};\beta}\right)\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\mathfrak{CoKer}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};\beta-1}(\widehat{D}^{0}_{\zeta})={\mathrm{coker}}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K};\beta-1}\right).

as the adiabatic kernel and adiabatic cokernel.

\lozenge

Definition 3.21
.

A resolution
\rho_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}:(N_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta},\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta},g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})\dashrightarrow(N_{0},\Phi_{0},g_{0})
is called isentropic if

\displaystyle{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta})\cong\mathfrak{Ker}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};\beta}(\widehat{D}^{0}_{\zeta})\oplus{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(S).

\lozenge

An isentropic process in statistical mechanics is an adiabatic process that is reversible. In the same manner an isentropic resolution can be modelled by its adiabatic limit.

The adiabatic residues have a geometric interpretation. Let
(N_{\zeta},g^{t}_{\zeta})
satisfy

(18)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta}/S)\cong\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta}/S).

The adiabatic residue
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{K};\beta}=\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{K};L^{2}}
of the Hodge-de Rham operator on
(N_{\zeta},g_{\zeta})
is given by the Gauß-Manin-Hodge-de Rham operator

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{GM}\colon\bigoplus_{p+q=\bullet}\Omega^{p}\left(S,\mathcal{H}^{q}(N_{\zeta}/S)\right)\rightarrow\bigoplus_{p+q=\bullet}\Omega^{p}\left(S,\mathcal{H}^{q}(N_{\zeta}/S)\right)

given by the Hodge-de Rham operator twisted by the flat Gauß-Manin connection on
\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta}/S)\rightarrow S
. In particular, this operator can be seen a folding of the elliptic complex induced by the local system

\displaystyle L^{2}GM^{\bullet}(\nu_{\zeta}:N_{\zeta}\rightarrow S)=(\Omega^{\bullet}(S,\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta}/S)),{\mathrm{d}}_{GM}).

By classical results of algebraic topology, we can identify the adiabatic kernel with the second page of the Leray-Serre-spectral sequence.

Theorem 3.1

(
[
BT+82
]
)
.

There exists a spectral sequence
E^{p,q}_{N}
associated to the double complex

\displaystyle(K^{\bullet_{1},\bullet_{2}},{\mathrm{d}})=(\check{C}^{\bullet_{1}}(\nu_{\zeta}^{-1}\mathcal{U},\Omega^{\bullet_{2}}),\delta+(-1)^{\bullet_{1}}{\mathrm{d}}_{dR})

converging to the cohomology of
N_{\zeta}
, i.e.
\underset{p+q=\bullet}{{\mathrm{Tot}}}\left(E^{p,q}_{\infty}\right)={\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta})
. Here
\mathcal{U}
is a good cover of
S
. Moreover, the second page of this spectral sequence can be identified with

\displaystyle(\check{{\mathrm{H}}}^{\bullet_{1}}(\nu_{\zeta}^{-1}\mathcal{U},{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet_{2}}(N_{\zeta}/S)),{\mathrm{d}}^{p,q}_{2}).

Proposition 3.3
.

Let us assume that
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{SU}}(m/2)
and
{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta}/S)\cong\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta}/S)
. There exists an isomorphism

\displaystyle E^{p,q}_{2}(N_{\zeta})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathrm{H}}^{p}(S)&\text{if }q=0\\
{\mathrm{H}}^{p}(S,\mathcal{H}^{q}(N_{\zeta}/S))&\text{else}\end{array}\right.

i.e.

\displaystyle E^{\bullet,\bullet}_{2}(N_{\zeta})\cong{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(S)\oplus{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(L^{2}GM^{\bullet}(\nu_{\zeta}:N_{\zeta}\rightarrow S)).

Moreover, for
0<N
the differentials
d_{2N}=0
. In particular,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Ker}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};\beta}(\widehat{D}^{0}_{\zeta})\cong L^{2}E^{\bullet,\bullet}_{2}(N_{\zeta}).

Notation
.

We will denote by

\displaystyle\beta_{i}(N_{\zeta})\coloneqq\sum_{p+q=i}{\mathrm{dim}}(E^{p,q}_{2}(N_{\zeta}))-b_{i}(S).

Corollary 3.2
.

A
\rho_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}:(N_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta},\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta},g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})\dashrightarrow(N_{0},\Phi_{0},g_{0})
is isentropic, if and only if the spectral sequence
E^{\bullet,\bullet}_{N}
collapses on the second page.

Definition 3.22
.

We define the approximate kernel and cokernel of
\widetilde{D}^{pre}_{\zeta;t}
by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{xker}}_{\beta}(\widetilde{D}^{pre}_{\zeta;t})=\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathrm{ker}}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{K};\beta}:\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{t}\left(S,\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(N_{\zeta}/S)\right)\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{t}\left(S,\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(N_{\zeta}/S)\right)\right)\\
\oplus\\
{\mathrm{ker}}\left(D\colon\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{CFS}};\beta;\epsilon}(X;{\mathrm{APS}})\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{CFS}};\beta-1;\epsilon}(X)\right)\end{array}

and

\displaystyle{\mathrm{xcoker}}_{\beta-1}(\widetilde{D}^{pre}_{\zeta;t})=\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathrm{coker}}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K};\beta-1}:\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{t}\left(S,\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(N_{\zeta}/S)\right)\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{t}\left(S,\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}_{{\mathrm{AC}};\beta}(N_{\zeta}/S)\right)\right)\\
\oplus\\
{\mathrm{coker}}\left(D\colon\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{CFS}};\beta;\epsilon}(X;{\mathrm{APS}})\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{CFS}};\beta-1;\epsilon}(X)\right).\end{array}

\lozenge

Before we state the main result of
[
Maj25b
]
we need to prove the following technical Lemma. This Lemma essentially ensures that the adiabatic limit of the operator
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}
is “well-behaved”, i.e. that a uniform elliptic estimate holds on the complement of the adiabatic kernel.

Lemma 3.6
.

Given
\Psi\in\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};\beta-1}(N_{\zeta})
, we can bound

\displaystyle\left|\left|L_{{\mathrm{AC}};-\beta-m-1}\{\widehat{D}_{\zeta;V};\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta;H}\}\pi_{\mathcal{K};\beta}\Psi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};\beta-1}}\lesssim t^{\phi}\cdot\left|\left|\pi_{\mathcal{K};\beta}\Psi\right|\right|_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};\beta}}.

for
\phi\geq 1
.

Proof.

Notice, that the de Rham differential
{\mathrm{d}}={\mathrm{d}}^{1,0}+{\mathrm{d}}^{0,1}+{\mathrm{d}}^{2,-1}
on a fibration satisfies the identities

\displaystyle\{{\mathrm{d}}^{1,0},{\mathrm{d}}^{1,0}\}=

\displaystyle-2\{{\mathrm{d}}^{0,1},{\mathrm{d}}^{2,-1}\},\hskip 8.5359pt\{{\mathrm{d}}^{0,1},{\mathrm{d}}^{0,1}\}=0,\hskip 8.5359pt\{{\mathrm{d}}^{2,-1},{\mathrm{d}}^{2,-1}\}=0

\displaystyle\{{\mathrm{d}}^{1,0},{\mathrm{d}}^{0,1}\}=

\displaystyle 0,\hskip 8.5359pt\{{\mathrm{d}}^{1,0},{\mathrm{d}}^{2,-1}\}=0.

The same identities hold for the codifferential
{\mathrm{d}}^{*}={\mathrm{d}}^{-1,0}+{\mathrm{d}}^{0,-1}+{\mathrm{d}}^{-2,1}
. The statement follows from a direct computation for
L_{{\mathrm{AC}};-\beta-m-1}\{\widehat{D}_{\zeta;V};\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta;H}\}
.
∎

Before we state the main result of
[
Maj25b
]
we need to prove the following technical Lemma. This Lemma essentially ensures that the adiabatic limit of the operator
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}
is “well-behaved”, i.e. that a uniform elliptic estimate holds on the complement of the adiabatic kernel.

Theorem 3.2
.

[
Maj25b
, Thm. 9.1]

Let
\beta\notin\mathcal{C}(\widehat{D}_{0})
and

\displaystyle\iota\geq

\displaystyle\phi-m/2-\beta\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\pi\geq\phi+m/2+\beta-1-\alpha

whereby
\phi\geq 1
. The map

(19)

\displaystyle D^{pre}_{\zeta;t}\colon\mathfrak{D}\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{\beta;t}(X_{\zeta;t})\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{\beta-1;t}(X_{\zeta;t})

is a bounded Fredholm map, whose kernel and cokernel satisfy

(20)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(X_{\zeta;t},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{xker}}_{\beta}(D^{pre}_{\zeta;t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{xcoker}}_{\beta-1}(D^{pre}_{\zeta;t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(X_{\zeta;t},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\beta;t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ob}}_{\beta;t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\beta;t}}

Furthermore, the uniform elliptic estimate

(21)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Psi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{D}^{k+1,\alpha}_{\beta;t}}\lesssim\left|\left|D^{pre}_{\zeta;t}\Psi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{C}^{k,\alpha}_{\beta-1;t}}

holds for all
\Psi\in{\mathrm{xker}}_{\beta}(D_{\zeta;t}^{pre})^{\perp}
. Hence, if
{\mathrm{ob}}_{\beta;t}=0
there exists uniform bounded map

(22)

\displaystyle R^{pre}_{\zeta;t}\colon\mathfrak{C}\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{\beta-1;t}(X_{\zeta;t},\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X_{\zeta;t})\rightarrow\mathfrak{D}\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{\beta;t}(X_{\zeta;t})

that restricts to a right-inverse.

So far we have only established uniform bounds for the right-inverse of the Hodge-de Rham operator for the naive-preglued-metric. However, the next Proposition ensures that the results extend to the Hodge-de Rham operator associated
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure
\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

[
Maj25b
, Prop. 9.4]

Let
\rho_{t}\colon(X_{t},g_{t})\dashrightarrow(X,g)
be a
(k+1)
-tame smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolution with respect to
\rho_{\zeta;t}\colon(X_{\zeta;t},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\dashrightarrow(X,g)
. The
L^{2}
-projection induces a natural identification

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(X_{\zeta;t},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\cong\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(X_{\zeta;t},g_{t}).

and Hodge theory yields a further identification

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(X_{\zeta;t},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\cong{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(X_{\zeta;t})\cong{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(X)\oplus{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta})/{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(S).

If moreover
{\mathrm{ob}}_{\beta;t}=0
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(X_{\zeta;t},g^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\cong{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(X)\oplus{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(S,\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(N_{\zeta}/S))

and
D_{t}
admits a uniform bounded right-inverse, i.e. there exists a map Fredholm map

(23)

\displaystyle R_{t}\colon\mathfrak{C}\Omega^{\bullet;k,\alpha}_{\beta-1;t}(X_{\zeta;t})\rightarrow\mathfrak{D}\Omega^{\bullet;k+1,\alpha}_{\beta;t}(X_{\zeta;t})

satisfying

\displaystyle\left|\left|R_{t}\Psi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{C}^{k+1,\alpha}_{\beta;t}}\lesssim\left|\left|\Psi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{C}^{k,\alpha}_{\beta-1;t}}.

Remark 12
.

By the exactness of (
20
) we know that an obstruction to
{\mathrm{ob}}_{\beta;t}=0
is the surjectivity of the map
\iota_{\beta;t}
. As
\iota_{\beta;t}
is injective, it is surjective if and only if
{\mathrm{dim}}\left(\mathcal{H}^{\bullet}(X_{\zeta;t},\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta:t})\right)={\mathrm{dim}}({\mathrm{xker}}_{\beta}(D^{pre}_{\zeta;t}))
. If
\beta<0
is larger than the biggest negative critical rate, then by Proposition
3.1
and (
20
) the former holds true if and only if the resolution
\rho_{t;\zeta}:(X_{\zeta,t},\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\dashrightarrow(X,\Phi)
is isentropic. By Corollary
3.2
this is equivalent to the collapsing of the Leray-Serre-spectral sequence on the second page.

4.
Adiabatic Spin(7)-Orbifold Resolutions

The goal of this section is to construct adiabatic smooth Gromov-Hausdorff resolutions of
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures. Building on the analytic framework developed in
[
Maj25b
]
, we will identify the local data needed to resolve CF-
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
normal cones along the singular strata of a
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold, and construct suitable ACF-
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
spaces using techniques from symplectic and algebraic geometry. By interpolating between these ACF-
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures and the
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold structure, we construct a family of
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures converging to the
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold, whose torsion vanishes in the orbifold limit.

We will begin by briefly sketching the general idea of the construction. Let
(X,\Phi)
be a
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold and let
D_{\Phi}\coloneqq{\mathrm{d}}+{\mathrm{d}}^{*_{g_{\Phi}}}=D
denote the Hodge-de Rham operator with respect to
g_{\Phi}
. We will think of the torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure as an element of

\displaystyle\Phi\in{\mathrm{ker}}_{{\mathrm{CFS}};0}(D_{\Phi})\cap\Gamma(X,Cay_{+}(Y))\subset\mathcal{H}^{4}(X,\Phi).

In
[
Maj25b
]
the author established that given a resolution
\rho_{\zeta;t}:(X_{\zeta;t},\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\dashrightarrow(X,\Phi)
and under the assumption
\rho_{\zeta;t}:(X_{\zeta;t},\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\dashrightarrow(X,\Phi)
being an isentropic resolution, i.e. the vanishing of the obstruction map
ob_{0,t}=0
, there exists an isomorphism

\displaystyle{\mathrm{ker}}(D_{\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}})\cong{\mathrm{xker}}_{0}(D^{pre}_{\zeta;t}).

Assume that there exists a torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure

\displaystyle\Phi_{\zeta;t}\in{\mathrm{ker}}(D_{\Phi_{\zeta;t}})\cap\Gamma(X_{\zeta;t},Cay_{+}(X_{\zeta;t}))

close to
\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}
, such that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{ker}}(D_{\Phi_{\zeta;t}})\cong{\mathrm{ker}}(D^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\cong{\mathrm{xker}}_{0}(D^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\cong\mathfrak{Ker}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};0}(\widehat{D}^{0}_{\zeta})\times_{{\mathrm{ker}}_{{\mathrm{CF}};0}(\widehat{D}_{0})}{\mathrm{ker}}_{{\mathrm{CFS}};0}(D).

The existence of such a
\Phi_{\zeta;t}
enables us to uniquely identify it with a pair

\displaystyle(\Phi_{\zeta}\times_{\Phi_{0}}\Phi)\in\mathfrak{Ker}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};0}(\widehat{D}^{0}_{\zeta})\times_{{\mathrm{ker}}_{{\mathrm{CF}};0}(\widehat{D}_{0})}{\mathrm{ker}}_{{\mathrm{CFS}};0}(D).

The element in
{\mathrm{ker}}_{{\mathrm{CFS}};0}(D)
is given by
\Phi
itself; as well as a

\displaystyle\Phi_{\zeta}\in\mathfrak{Ker}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};0}(\widehat{D}_{\zeta})\cap\Gamma(N_{\zeta},Cay_{+}(N_{\zeta}))

resolving the normal cone structure

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}\in{\mathrm{ker}}_{CF,0}(\widehat{D}_{0})\cap\Gamma(N_{0},Cay_{+}(N_{0})).

Hence, in order to construct a
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold resolution, we will need to construct ACF-
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures

(24)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\zeta}\in\mathfrak{Ker}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};0}(\widehat{D}^{0}_{\zeta})\cap\Gamma(N_{\zeta},Cay_{+}(N_{\zeta}))

asymptotic to
(N_{0},\Phi_{0})
.

We will construct such ACF spaces as moduli spaces of vertical Hermitian Yang–Mills instantons or
\Gamma
-constellations on the normal cone, depending on harmonic sections of certain parameter bundles of stability conditions arising from GIT-quotients. We then solve the nonlinear gluing problem using the analytic theory developed in
[
Maj25b
]
, culminating in a new existence theorem for
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold resolutions. Applications include explicit constructions of compact
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-manifolds and extensions to the resolution of singularities in
G_{2}
-orbifolds.

4.1.
Spin(7)-Normal Cones

Let
(X,\Phi)
be a
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold and
S\subset X
a singular strata. Recall, that the normal cone structure
\Phi_{0}
of
\Phi
with respect to
S
is the CF-
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure on the normal cone
\nu_{0}:N_{0}\rightarrow S
induced by the restriction of
\Phi
to
S
. Given a tubular neighbourhood
{\mathrm{exp}}_{g_{\Phi}}\colon{\mathrm{Tub}}_{5\epsilon}(S)\hookrightarrow X
, we can identify
\Phi_{0}
as the leading order term, in an expansion

\displaystyle{\mathrm{exp}}_{g_{\Phi}}^{*}\Phi=\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{hot}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt|\Phi_{hot}|_{g_{\Phi_{0}}}=\mathcal{O}(r)

in terms of the distance
r
to the singular stratum. In the following section we will analyse the structure
\Phi_{0}
in more detail. We will show that it defines an element in

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}\in{\mathrm{ker}}_{CF,0}(\widehat{D}_{0})\cap\Gamma(N_{0},Cay_{+}(N_{0}))

and relate this behaviour to Donaldson’s notion of adiabatic torsion-free, fibred, exceptional holonomy metrics
[
Don16
]
.

Remark 13
.

Recall that the projection map

\displaystyle\nu_{0}:(N_{0},\Phi_{0})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})

has totally geodesic fibres. Furthermore, the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{g_{\Phi}}
on
X
induces a connection
\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}
on
N_{0}
that is metric with respect to
g_{0}
and whose torsion can be identified with the curvature of the Ehresmann connection
H_{0}
. The Levi-Civita connection
\nabla^{g_{\Phi}}
expands as

\displaystyle\nabla^{g_{\Phi}}=\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}+\mathcal{O}(r).

The connection
\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}=\nu_{0}^{*}\nabla^{g_{s}}\oplus\nabla^{\nu}
is given by the direct sum connection of the Levi-Civita connection on
S
lifted along
H_{0}
and the fibrewise flat connection.

We will continue by analysing the normal cone structures depending on the codimension of the singular stratum.

Recall from
3.1.1
that there exists a canonical
{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}
-reduction of the
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-frame bundle

\displaystyle\phi:Fr(X/S,\Phi)\rightarrow S

of frames normalising the action of the isotropy group
\Gamma
.

Remark 14
.

In the presence of an Ehresmann connection, the de Rham differential decomposes into

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}={\mathrm{d}}^{1,0}+{\mathrm{d}}^{0,1}+{\mathrm{d}}^{2,-1}.

Lemma 4.1

(Codimension Four)
.

Let
S\subset X^{sing}
be a singular strata of codimension four, isotropy
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{SU}}(2)={\mathrm{Sp}}(1)
. There exist representations

\displaystyle\varrho_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}(\Gamma)\rightarrow{\mathrm{SO}}(\mathbb{R}^{4})

\displaystyle\varrho_{\mathbb{H}/\Gamma}:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}(\Gamma)\rightarrow{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{SO}}(\mathbb{H})}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\varrho_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{H})}:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}(\Gamma)\rightarrow{\mathrm{SO}}({\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{H})

such that

\displaystyle TS\cong

\displaystyle Fr(X/S,\Phi)\times_{{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}(\Gamma)}\mathbb{R}^{4}

\displaystyle N_{0}\cong

\displaystyle Fr(X/S,\Phi)\times_{{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}(\Gamma)}\mathbb{H}/\Gamma

\displaystyle\wedge^{2}_{+}T^{*}S\cong

\displaystyle Fr(X/S,\Phi)\times_{{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}(\Gamma)}{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{H})^{*}.

Let
\left[\theta_{S}\wedge\theta_{S}\right]_{+}\in\Omega^{2}_{hor,+}(Fr(X/S,\Phi),{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{H})^{*})^{{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}(\Gamma)}
be the self-dual Soldering form corresponding to the isomorphism
Fr(X/S,\Phi)\times_{{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}(\Gamma)}{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{H})^{*}\cong\wedge^{2}_{+}T^{*}S
. The CF-
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure on the normal cone can be expressed as

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}=\Phi^{4,0}_{0}+\Phi^{2,2}_{0}+\Phi^{0,4}_{0}

whereby lifted to
Fr(X/S,\Phi)\times\mathbb{H}/\Gamma
the components can be expressed as

\displaystyle\hat{\Phi}^{4,0}_{0}=\phi^{*}{\mathrm{vol}}_{g_{S}},\hskip 28.45274pt\hat{\Phi}^{2,2}_{0}=-{\mathrm{tr}}(\left[\theta_{S}\wedge\theta_{S}\right]_{+}\wedge\underline{\omega}_{0})\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\hat{\Phi}^{0,4}_{0}=\frac{1}{6}{\mathrm{tr}}(\underline{\omega}_{0}\wedge\underline{\omega}_{0}).

Here
\underline{\omega}_{0}
denotes the standard
{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
-structure on
\mathbb{H}
and
\left[\theta_{S}\wedge\theta_{S}\right]_{+}
the self-dual Soldering two form. Moreover, the CF-
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure satisfies

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{0}={\mathrm{d}}^{2,-1}\Phi_{0}

or equivalently

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi_{0}=0.

Lemma 4.2

(Codimension Six)
.

Let
S\subset X^{sing}
be a singular strata of codimension six, isotropy
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{SU}}(3)
. There exist representations

\displaystyle\varrho_{\mathbb{C}}:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}(\Gamma)\rightarrow{\mathrm{SO}}(\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle\varrho_{\mathbb{C}^{3}/\Gamma}:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}(\Gamma)\rightarrow{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{SO}}(\mathbb{C}^{3})}(\Gamma)

such that

\displaystyle TS\cong

\displaystyle Fr(X/S,\Phi)\times_{{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}(\Gamma)}\mathbb{R}^{2}

\displaystyle N_{0}\cong

\displaystyle Fr(X/S,\Phi)\times_{{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}(\Gamma)}\mathbb{C}^{3}/\Gamma

Let
\theta_{S}\in\Omega^{1}_{hor,+}(Fr(X/S,\Phi),\mathbb{C})^{{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}(\Gamma)}
be the Soldering form corresponding to the isomorphism
Fr(X/S,\Phi)\times_{{\mathrm{N}}_{{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)}(\Gamma)}\mathbb{C}^{*}\cong T^{*}S
. The CF-
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure on the normal cone can be expressed as

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}=\Phi^{2,2}_{0}+\Phi^{1,3}_{0}+\Phi^{0,4}_{0}

whereby lifted to
Fr(X/S,\Phi)\times\mathbb{C}^{3}/\Gamma
the components can be expressed as

\displaystyle\hat{\Phi}^{2,2}_{0}=\phi^{*}{\mathrm{vol}}_{g_{S}}\wedge\omega_{0},\hskip 28.45274pt\hat{\Phi}^{1,3}_{0}=-{\mathrm{tr}}(\theta_{S}\wedge\theta_{0})\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\hat{\Phi}^{0,4}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{tr}}(\omega_{0}\wedge\omega_{0}).

Here
(\omega_{0},\theta_{0})
denotes the standard
{\mathrm{SU}}(3)
-structure on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Moreover, the CF-
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure satisfies

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{0}={\mathrm{d}}^{2,-1}\Phi_{0}.

or equivalently

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi_{0}=0.

For codimension seven and eight singular strata, the CF-
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures are trivial in the following sense. The normal cone bundle of codimension seven singular strata is trivialisable, i.e.

\displaystyle N_{0}\cong\mathbb{T}^{1}\times{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{O})

and the normal cone structure is given by

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}=\Phi_{0}^{1,3}+\Phi_{0}^{0,4}=\theta\wedge\phi_{0}+\psi_{0}

where
(\phi_{0},\psi_{0})
is the standard
G_{2}
-structure on
{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{O})
. For codimension eight-singular strata, i.e. conical singularities the normal cone structure is given by the standard
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}=\left<.,.\times_{0}.\times_{0}.\right>_{0}.

on
\mathbb{O}/\Gamma
.

As
\Phi
is assumed to be a torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure, i.e.
{\mathrm{d}}\Phi=0
, we are able to deduce the following properties of
\Phi_{hot}
that hold regardless of the chosen tubular neighbourhood
j\colon{\mathrm{Tub}}_{3\epsilon}(S)\rightarrow X
. These properties enable the construction of torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold resolutions and make the construction of “special tubular neighbourhoods” as in
[
JK21
]
obsolete.

The Ehresmann connection
H_{0}
on
N_{0}
induces an Ehresmann connection on the tubular neighbourhood of the singular stratum. The following lemma will compute the “torsion” of the higher-order terms in the expansion of
\Phi
by using the torsion-free ness of
\Phi
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
{\mathrm{exp}}_{g_{\Phi}}\colon{\mathrm{Tub}}_{5\epsilon}(S)\hookrightarrow(X,\Phi)
be the exponential neighbourhood of
S
with respect to
g_{\Phi}
and let
\Phi
be torsion-free. Then
\Phi_{hot}
satisfies

(25)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{hot}=-{\mathrm{d}}^{2,-1}\Phi_{0}.

Further, let
\Phi_{hot}=\Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2\leq}
. Then

(26)

\displaystyle\pi_{\tau;\Phi_{0}}\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{1}.

Proof.

The first property follows form
0={\mathrm{exp}}_{g_{\Phi}}^{*}{\mathrm{d}}\Phi={\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{exp}}_{g_{\Phi}}^{*}\Phi={\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{0}+{\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{hot}
and the fact that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{0}={\mathrm{d}}^{2,-1}\Phi_{0}.

as
S
is totally geodesic with respect to
\Phi
. For the second property we use that
\Phi_{0}\in\mathcal{C}ay_{+}(N_{0})
and
\Theta(\Phi)=\Phi

\displaystyle\Theta(\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2})=

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}+\pi_{\tau;\Phi_{0}}\Phi_{1}+\pi_{\tau;\Phi_{0}}\Phi_{2}+Q_{\Phi_{0}}(\Phi_{1}+\Phi_{2}).

Analysing order by order in
r
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\pi_{\tau;\Phi_{0}}\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{1}.

∎

We will continue by discussing the notion of fibred
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures and the notion of adiabatic torsion-freeness as introduced by Donaldson in
[
Don16
]
. We will see that the
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-normal cone structure of a singular stratum naturally defines an adiabatic torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure. Later in this section, we will construct resolutions of the normal cone bundle and we will see that the notion of torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures is the right setting for the construction of resolutions of
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifolds.

Let
\pi:(X,\Phi)\rightarrow(B,g_{B})
be a
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure inducing a Riemannian fibration over
(B,g_{B})
. The Riemannian structure induced by
\Phi
induces an Ehresmann connection
H
such that

\displaystyle g_{\Phi}=g^{2,0}_{\Phi}+g^{0,2}_{\Phi}=\pi^{*}g_{B}\oplus g^{0,2}_{\Phi}

and
\Phi=\sum_{k=0}^{4}\Phi^{k,4-k}
. We define a family of
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures
\Phi^{t}
on
X
by

\displaystyle\Phi^{t}\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{4}t^{k}\Phi^{4-k,k}\Rightarrow g_{\Phi^{t}}=g^{2,0}_{\Phi}+t^{2}g^{0,2}_{\Phi}

which geometrically corresponds to scaling the fibres by
t
. Donaldson’s program on adiabatic torsion-free exceptional holonomy spaces aims at understanding the adiabatic limit of special holonomy structures, extracting the adiabatic special holonomy data and constructing torsion-free special holonomy spaces given such an adiabatic datum.

Definition 4.1
.

A fibred
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure
\pi:(X,\Phi)\rightarrow(B,g_{B})
is called
adiabatic torsion-free
if

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\Phi^{t}=\mathcal{O}(t),

i.e. the family
\Phi^{t}
is formally torsion-free in the adiabatic limit
\lim_{t\to 0}(X,\Phi^{t})
.

\lozenge

Lemma 4.4
.

A fibred
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure
\pi:(X,\Phi)\rightarrow(B,g_{B})
is adiabatic torsion-free if and only if

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\Phi={\mathrm{d}}^{2,-1}\Phi.

Proof.

In the presence of the Ehresmann connection induced by
\Phi
, the de Rham differential splits into

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}={\mathrm{d}}^{1,0}+{\mathrm{d}}^{0,1}+{\mathrm{d}}^{2,-1}.

We compute

\displaystyle\left|{\mathrm{d}}\Phi^{t}\right|_{g_{\Phi^{t}}}^{2}=\left|{\mathrm{d}}^{1,0}\Phi+t^{-1}\cdot{\mathrm{d}}^{0,1}\Phi+t\cdot{\mathrm{d}}^{2,-1}\Phi\right|_{g_{\Phi}}^{2}

which only satisfies
\mathcal{O}(t)
if
{\mathrm{d}}^{1,0}\Phi=0
and
{\mathrm{d}}^{0,1}\Phi=0
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

[
Goe14
, Eq. (2.3)]

Let
\pi:(X,\Phi)\rightarrow(B,g_{B})
be a fibred
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure with minimal fibres. Then
\Phi
is adiabatic torsion-free if and only if

\displaystyle\widehat{D}\Phi\coloneqq{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{\Phi}}\circ\nabla^{\oplus}\Phi=0

where
{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{\Phi}}:T^{*}X\otimes\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X\rightarrow\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X
denote the Clifford multiplication.

4.2.
Resolutions of Spin(7)-Orbifolds with Type A Singular Strata

In the following section we will describe the data needed to resolve a
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold with a type A singular strata.

Assumption 1

(Type A)
.

Let us assume that there exists a family of torsion-free ACF-
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-spaces
(N_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta},\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})
resolving the family of CF
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold
(N_{0},\Phi_{0})
, i.e. there exists maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N_{\zeta},\Phi^{t}_{\zeta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta},\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N_{0},\Phi^{t}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(N_{0},\Phi_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\zeta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{t}}

whose exceptional set
\rho_{\zeta}^{-1}(0)=\Upsilon_{\zeta}
is of codimension
>0
such that

\displaystyle\Phi_{\zeta}\in\mathfrak{Ker}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};0}(\widehat{D}_{\zeta})\cap\Gamma(N_{\zeta},Cay_{+}(N_{\zeta}).

In particular, the torsion of
\Phi^{t}_{\zeta}
satisfies both

\displaystyle\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\xrightarrow[C^{\infty}_{loc}]{t\to 0}\Phi_{0}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt{\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\xrightarrow[C^{\infty}_{loc}]{t\to 0}{\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{0}\,\text{ on }N_{\zeta}\backslash\Upsilon_{\zeta}.

The Riemannian structure associated to
\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}
decomposes into

\displaystyle\delta^{*}_{t}g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}=\nu_{\zeta}^{*}g_{S}+t^{2}\cdot g_{\zeta}.

Moreover
\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}
is of rate
\upsilon=-m
, i.e.

\displaystyle(\nabla^{\Phi^{t}_{V,\nu}})^{k}((\rho_{\zeta})_{*}\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta;V}-\Phi_{0;V})=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{-m-k})

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{0}})^{k}((\rho_{\zeta})_{*}\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{-m}).

We interpolate between the torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
orbifold structure on
X
and the adiabatic torsion-free ACF
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structure on
B_{3\epsilon}(N_{\zeta})
using the cut-off function
{\chi_{3}}
, i.e.

(27)

\displaystyle\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}=\Theta(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\cup_{t}\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{hot})=\Theta(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\cup_{t}\Phi+(1-\chi_{3})\Phi_{hot}).

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\rho_{\zeta;t}\colon(X_{\zeta;t},\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\dashrightarrow(X,\Phi)
be a Riemannian orbifold resolution constructed using the data given by Assumption
1
. Then

\displaystyle\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Phi+(1-\chi_{3})\pi_{\tau,\Phi}(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})+Q_{\Phi}((1-\chi_{3})(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0}))&\text{on }X_{\zeta;t}\backslash\Upsilon_{\zeta;t}\\
\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}+\chi_{3}\pi_{\tau,\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})+\pi_{\tau,\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}(\Phi_{hot})&\text{on }U_{2\epsilon}\\
+Q_{\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}(\chi_{3}(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})+\Phi_{hot})&\end{array}\right.

the torsion of
\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}
satisfies

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}\xrightarrow[C^{\infty}_{loc}]{t\to 0}0

and hence,
\rho_{\zeta;t}\colon(X_{\zeta;t},\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\dashrightarrow(X,\Phi)
is an adiabatic torsion-free
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold resolution.

Proof.

Define
\widetilde{\Phi}^{pre}_{\zeta;t}\coloneqq\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\cup_{t}\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{hot}
and let
\phi_{t}
denote the “small” four form

\displaystyle\phi_{t}\coloneqq\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}-\widetilde{\Phi}^{pre}_{\zeta;t}

We compute

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\Phi}^{pre}_{\zeta;t}=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\left(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\cup_{t}\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{hot}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\left(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\cup_{t}\Phi+(1-\chi_{3})\Phi_{hot}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\cup_{t}0+{\mathrm{d}}\chi_{3}\wedge(\Phi-\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{hot})+(1-\chi_{3})\Phi_{hot}

but since,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\Phi=0\Leftrightarrow{\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{hot}=-{\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{0}

we deduce that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\Phi}^{pre}_{\zeta;t}={\mathrm{d}}(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})-{\mathrm{d}}\chi_{3}\wedge(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0}).

Notice, that the “torsion” of
\widetilde{\Phi}^{pre}_{\zeta;t}
does not depend on
j
and we deduce that

\displaystyle|{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\Phi}^{pre}_{\zeta;t}|_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}\leq

\displaystyle|{\mathrm{d}}(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})|_{g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}+\mathcal{O}(t^{m-(m-1)\lambda})

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle|{\mathrm{d}}(\Phi^{t}_{\zeta}-\Phi^{t}_{0})|_{g^{t}_{\zeta}}+\mathcal{O}(t^{m-(m-1)\lambda})

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle t\cdot|{\mathrm{d}}(\Phi_{\zeta}-\Phi_{0})|_{g_{\zeta}}=\mathcal{O}(t).

To bound the
{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{t}
we compute

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{t}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-(1-\chi_{3}){\mathrm{d}}\pi_{\nu,\Phi}(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})-{\mathrm{d}}\chi_{3}\wedge\pi_{\nu,\Phi}(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})&\text{on }X_{\zeta;t}\backslash\Upsilon_{\zeta;t}\\
+{\mathrm{d}}Q_{\Phi}((1-\chi_{3})(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0}))&\\
(1-\chi_{3}){\mathrm{d}}\pi_{\nu,\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}(\Phi_{hot})-\chi_{3}{\mathrm{d}}\pi_{\nu,\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})&\text{on }U_{2\epsilon}\\
-{\mathrm{d}}\chi_{3}\wedge\pi_{\nu,\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi)&\\
+{\mathrm{d}}Q_{\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}(\chi_{3}(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})+(1-\chi_{3})\Phi_{hot})&\end{array}\right.

and deduce that

\displaystyle|{\mathrm{d}}\phi_{t}|_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}\lesssim|(1-\chi_{3}){\mathrm{d}}\pi_{\nu,\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}(\Phi_{hot})|_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}.

using

\displaystyle\Theta(\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{hot})=\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{hot}\Leftrightarrow\pi_{\nu;\Phi_{0}}\Phi_{hot}=-Q_{\Phi_{0}}(\Phi_{hot}).

Hence,

\displaystyle|(1-\chi_{3}){\mathrm{d}}\pi_{\nu,\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}(\Phi_{hot})|_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}\lesssim

\displaystyle|(1-\chi_{3}){\mathrm{d}}\left(\pi_{\nu,\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}-\pi_{\nu,\Phi_{0}}\right)(\Phi_{hot})|_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}

\displaystyle+|(1-\chi_{3}){\mathrm{d}}Q_{\Phi_{0}}(\Phi_{hot})|_{g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}

and since

\displaystyle\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\xrightarrow[C^{\infty}_{loc}]{t\to 0}\Phi_{0}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt{\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\xrightarrow[C^{\infty}_{loc}]{t\to 0}{\mathrm{d}}\Phi_{0}\text{ on }N_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\backslash\Upsilon_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}

we conclude that

\displaystyle|{\mathrm{d}}\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}|=\mathcal{O}(t^{\lambda})

and therefore
{\mathrm{d}}\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}\xrightarrow[C^{\infty}_{loc}]{t\to 0}0
.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}\coloneqq g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\cup_{t}g_{0}+g_{hot}
be the Riemannian metric on
X_{t;\zeta}
constructed via interpolating the Riemannian orbifold structure and the ACF-structure on
N_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}
. Then
g_{\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}
is
1
-tame with respect to
g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\left|\left|\cup_{t}(g_{\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}-g^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\right|\right|_{C^{1}_{{\mathrm{ACF}};-1;t}\oplus C^{1}_{{\mathrm{CFS}};-1;\epsilon}}\lesssim t^{\lambda}.

Proof.

The map
\gamma\colon Cay_{+}\rightarrow Met_{+}
is analytic and hence, by using the expansion of
\Theta
we deduce that

\displaystyle\gamma(\Phi)=

\displaystyle\gamma(\Phi_{0})+T_{\Phi_{0}}\gamma(\pi_{\tau;\Phi_{0}}\Phi_{hot})+h.o.t.

\displaystyle\gamma(\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t})=

\displaystyle\gamma(\Phi)+T_{\Phi}\gamma\left(\pi_{\tau;\Phi}\left((1-\chi_{3})(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0}\right))\right)+h.o.t.

\displaystyle\gamma(\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t})=

\displaystyle\gamma(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})+T_{\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}\gamma\left(\pi_{\tau;\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}\left(\chi_{3}(\Phi_{0}-\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})+\Phi_{hot}\right)\right)+h.o.t.

Then

\displaystyle g_{\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}-g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}=

\displaystyle\gamma(\Phi_{\zeta;t})-\gamma(\Phi)-(1-\chi_{3})(\gamma(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})-\gamma(\Phi_{0}))+h.o.t.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\chi_{3})\left\{T_{\Phi}\gamma\left(\pi_{\tau;\Phi}\left(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0}\right)\right)-\gamma(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})-\gamma(\Phi_{0})\right\}+h.o.t.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\chi_{3})\left\{T_{\Phi}\gamma\circ\pi_{\tau;\Phi}-T_{\Phi_{t;s}}\gamma\right\}(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})+h.o.t.

where
\Phi_{t;s}=s\Phi_{0}+(1-s)(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})
for some
s\in(0,1)
. In the same way we have

\displaystyle g_{\Phi_{\zeta;t}}-g^{pre}_{\zeta;t}=

\displaystyle\gamma(\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t})-\gamma(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})-\chi_{3}(\gamma(\Phi_{0})-\gamma(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}))+\gamma(\Phi)_{hot}+h.o.t.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi_{3}\left\{T_{\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}\gamma\left(\pi_{\tau;\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}\left(\Phi_{0}-\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\right)\right)-\gamma(\Phi_{0})-\gamma(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})\right\}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle T_{\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}\gamma(\pi_{\tau;\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}\Phi_{hot})-\gamma(\Phi)_{hot}+h.o.t.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi_{3}\left\{T_{\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}\circ\pi_{\tau;\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}\gamma-T_{\Phi_{s}}\gamma\right\}(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle T_{\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}\gamma(\pi_{\tau;\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}\Phi_{hot})-T_{\Phi_{0}}\gamma(\pi_{\tau;\Phi_{0}}\Phi_{hot})+h.o.t.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi_{3}\left\{T_{\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}\gamma\circ\pi_{\tau;\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}-T_{\Phi_{s}}\gamma\right\}(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left\{\partial_{t;\sigma}T_{\Phi_{\sigma}}\gamma\circ\pi_{\tau;\Phi_{\sigma}}(\Phi_{hot})\right\}(s)(\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-\Phi_{0})+h.o.t.

where
\Phi_{\sigma}
is a path of Cayley forms connecting
\Phi_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}
and
\Phi_{0}
.
∎

By now using the
1
-tameness of
g_{\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}
Proposition
3.4
implies the following corollary.

Corollary 4.1
.

If
\rho_{\zeta;t}\colon(X_{\zeta;t},\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t})\dashrightarrow(X,\Phi)
is isentropic, the Hodge-de Rham operator
D_{\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}
admits a right-inverse satisfying

\displaystyle\left|\left|R_{\Phi^{pre}_{\zeta;t}}\Psi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{D}^{1,\alpha}_{\beta;t}}\lesssim\left|\left|\Psi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{C}^{0,\alpha}_{\beta;t}}.

4.3.
GIT, Kähler and HyperKähler Quotients

In the following subsection we will briefly discuss various methods used in algebraic and symplectic geometry to construct quotients of spaces under group actions. These construction methods are the Geometric Invariant Theory (GIT) quotient and the (hyper)Kähler quotient, which coincide in certain cases by the Kempf-Ness theorem
[
KN79
]
and produce families of quotients depending on certain stability conditions.

4.3.1.
GIT Quotients

In the following we will briefly outline the construction method of quotients using the Geometric Invariant Theory. The so called GIT-quotient plays an essential role in modern algebraic geometry. This algebraic method constructs universal families of quotients parameterised by a stratified space of stability conditions. We will follow the work of
[
Tho05
,
CI04
,
DW+16
,
Inf96
]
.

To set up the GIT quotient rigorously, we begin by recalling the notion of a linearisation of the group action with respect to an ample line bundle, which encodes the choice of stability condition. This forms the algebraic backbone of GIT and determines which orbits admit well-behaved quotients.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
X
be a quasi-projective scheme over a field
k
and
G
a reductive group acting on
X
. Let
L
be an ample line bundle on
X
equipped with a lift
\varrho_{\zeta}:L\rightarrow L
of the
G
-action, which will be referred to as a
G
-linearisation with respect to
L
or a
stability condition
, and we will denote it by
L_{\zeta}
. We will write
\Theta_{L}
for the set of all such stability conditions with respect to
L
.

\lozenge

Having fixed a stability condition via a linearised line bundle, we can now distinguish between semi-stable and stable points of the action. These subsets stratify the space and control the nature of the quotient. The stable locus behaves well under the group action and leads to a geometric quotient.

Definition 4.3
.

A point
x\in X
is called
semi-stable
if there exists a
G
-invariant section of
n\cdot L_{\zeta}
that is nonvanishing at
x
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The subset of semi-stable points
X^{ss}(\zeta)\subset X
with respect to the stability condition
\zeta
is an open subset of
X
. We further define a
G
-equivalence relation on
X^{ss}(\zeta)
by

\displaystyle x\sim y\hskip 28.45274pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 28.45274ptG.x\cap G.y\neq\emptyset.

A point
x\in X^{ss}(\zeta)
is called
stable
if it has a full dimensional closed orbit in
X^{ss}(\zeta)
. Moreover, the set of stable points
X^{s}(\zeta)\subset X^{ss}(\zeta)
forms an open subset.

\lozenge

The quotient is accompanied by a natural surjective map from the semi-stable locus. Importantly, this map restricts to a geometric quotient on the stable locus, which embeds openly into the GIT quotient space.

Proposition 4.1
.

There exists a
G
-invariant quotient map

\displaystyle X^{ss}(\zeta)\twoheadrightarrow X\sslash_{\zeta}G.

The space
X\sslash_{\zeta}G
is referred to as the GIT-quotient of
X
by
G
with respect to the stability condition
\zeta
. Moreover, this map restricts to an open subset

\displaystyle X^{s}(\zeta)/G\subset X\sslash_{\zeta}G.

If
X
is projective, the GIT quotient of
X
by
G
with respect to an ample
L_{\zeta}
is the projective variety

\displaystyle X\sslash_{\zeta}G={\mathrm{Proj}}\left(\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathrm{H}}^{0}\left(X,n\cdot L_{\zeta}\right)^{G}\right).

We now describe how GIT quotients can vary with the choice of linearisation, especially when
X
is affine. This leads to a family of quotients parameterised by characters of
G
, and these quotients are connected via morphisms that reflect changes in stability.

Theorem 4.1
.

[
Inf96
, Thm. 1.2]

Let us assume that
X
is an affine variety, equipped with a linear
G
action. Let
L=\mathcal{O}_{X}
be the trivial line bundle. A
G
-linearisation on
L
corresponds to a character
\chi_{\zeta}:G\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. There exists a projective morphism

\displaystyle\rho_{\zeta}:X\sslash_{\zeta}G\rightarrow X\sslash_{0}G

that restricts to an isomorphism over
\pi^{-1}_{\zeta}(X^{s}(0))
.

We now turn to the differential-geometric counterpart of the GIT quotient, which arises in the setting of Kähler and hyperkähler manifolds. These constructions rely on moment maps and symplectic reductions, and they often coincide with the GIT-quotient via the Kempf-Ness correspondence.

4.3.2.
Kähler and HyperKähler Quotients

In the following section we will briefly recall basic aspects of Kähler and hyperkähler quotients by following the work of Hitchin, Karlhede, Lindström, Ro
\check{{\mathrm{c}}}
ek
[
HKLR87
]
, Duistermaat
[
DH82
]
and Gocho
[
GN92
]
.

Let in the following
(X,\hat{\omega})
and
(X,\hat{\underline{\omega}})
be a Kähler and a hyperkähler manifold, and
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}
or
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{H}
respectively. Recall that a group
G
acts on
X
in a Hamiltonian or respectively trihamiltonian way, if there exists a (hyper)Kähler moment map

\displaystyle\mu:X\rightarrow{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}

satisfying
\left<T\mu,\xi\right>=\iota_{K^{\xi}}\hat{\omega}
or
\left<T\mu,\zeta\otimes\xi\right>=\iota_{K^{\xi}}\left<\underline{\hat{\omega}},\zeta\right>
.

Before constructing the quotient, it is crucial to identify the regular points of the moment map, as they control the local structure of the quotient. In particular, we recall that regularity corresponds to having discrete stabilisers under the group action.

Lemma 4.8
.

A point
x\in X
is a regular point if and only if its stabilizer
{\mathrm{Stab}}_{x}(G)
is discrete.

Next, we examine the linear algebra of the quotient map in terms of the moment map differential and the group action. This yields a canonical decomposition of the tangent space which plays a central role in constructing the quotient structure.

Proposition 4.2
.

[
HKLR87
]

Let
x\in X
and define

\displaystyle V_{x}=\{K^{\xi}_{x}|\xi\in\mathfrak{g}\}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274ptK_{x}={\mathrm{ker}}T_{x}\mu\cap V_{x}^{\perp}.

Moreover,
V_{x}\oplus({\mathrm{ker}}T_{x}\mu)^{\perp}=\mathbb{K}\cdot V_{x}
and
K_{x}
are perpendicular
\mathbb{K}
-modules.

Let
\mathfrak{z}^{*}
be the annihilator of
[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]
, i.e. the centre of the Lie algebra of
\mathfrak{g}
. We will denote the space
{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{z}^{*}
by
\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})}
and let
\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})}^{reg}\subset\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})}
denote the subset of regular values of the moment maps.

With these preliminaries, we can now describe the local and global structure of the reduced spaces
M_{\zeta}
, obtained via the (hyper)Kähler quotient. These spaces inherit natural geometric structures and often vary smoothly in families indexed by the choice of moment map level
\zeta
. Let us assume that
G
acts properly on
\mu^{-1}(\zeta)
for all
\zeta\in\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})}
.

Proposition 4.3
.

[
HKLR87
, Thm. 3.1, Thm. 3.2]
[
GN92
, Thm. 1.3]

Let
\zeta\in\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})}^{reg}
and set
P_{\zeta}=\mu^{-1}(\zeta)
and
M_{\zeta}\coloneqq P_{\zeta}/G

•

For
p\in P_{\zeta}
,
\mathrm{Stab}_{p}(G)
is finite and hence,
M_{\zeta}
is an orbifold and
\mathfrak{p}_{\zeta}:P_{\zeta}\rightarrow M_{\zeta}
is a principal
G
-orbifold bundle

•

V_{p}=V_{p}\mathfrak{p}_{\zeta}

•

K_{\zeta}=\bigcup_{p\in P_{\zeta}}K_{p}
defines a natural principal
G
-connection on
P_{\zeta}

•

Since
G
acts via symplectomorphism or hyperplectomorphisms respectively,
\hat{\omega}|_{P_{\zeta}}
or
\hat{\underline{\omega}}|_{P_{\zeta}}
are basic. Consequently, they descend to a Kähler structure
{\omega}_{\zeta}
or a hyperkähler structure
\underline{\omega}_{\zeta}
on
M_{\zeta}

•

F_{\mathbb{A}_{\zeta}}\in\Omega^{2}_{hor}(P_{\zeta},\mathfrak{g})^{G}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left<F_{\mathbb{A}_{\zeta}},\omega_{\zeta}\right>=0\hskip 28.45274pt\text{or}\hskip 28.45274pt\left<F_{\mathbb{A}_{\zeta}},\underline{\omega}_{\zeta}\right>=0.

To globalise the quotient construction, we introduce a universal family of (hyper)Kähler quotients parameterised by the space of all stability conditions. This yields a natural geometric fibration equipped with universal connections and forms.

Definition 4.4
.

We define the
universal family of (hyper)Kähler quotients

\mathbb{M}
and the universal principal
G
-bundle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{pr}}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}=\mu^{-1}(\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})})\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{P}/G.

\lozenge

Remark 15
.

Notice, that the space
\mathbb{M}
is a stratified space and the map
\kappa:\mathbb{M}\rightarrow\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})}
is a map of stratified spaces. It restricts to a smooth fibre bundle on the regular strata.

The universal family satisfies several important differential-geometric properties that are crucial for understanding the variation of the reduced structures. In particular, we describe the behaviour of tangent bundles, connections, and curvature, as well as the dependence of the quotient geometry on the parameters.

Proposition 4.4
.

[
HKLR87
, Thm. 3.1, Thm. 3.2]
[
GN92
, Thm. 1.3]

The universal family of (hyper)Kähler quotients
\kappa\colon\mathbb{M}\rightarrow\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})}
and the universal principal bundle
\mathfrak{p}\colon\mathbb{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{M}
satisfy the following properties

•

The tangent bundle of
X
at
\mathbb{P}
decomposes into

\displaystyle TX|_{\mathbb{P}}=K\oplus\hat{H}\oplus V

where

\displaystyle V=V\mathfrak{p}\hskip 28.45274pt\mathbb{K}=\mathrm{ker}(T\mu)\cap V^{\perp}\hskip 28.45274pt\hat{H}=\mathrm{ker}(T\mu)^{\perp}.

Furthermore,
K\oplus\hat{H}
defines a natural principal
G
-connection on
\mathfrak{p}
and the
G
invariant connection
\hat{H}
on
\kappa\circ\mathfrak{p}=\mu
which descends to a connection
\check{H}
on
\kappa
. Moreover, the vertical subbundle of
\mu
lifts to
K
. We will denote the connection one form of
K\oplus\hat{H}
by
\mathbb{A}\in\mathcal{A}(\mathfrak{p})

•

The 2-form
({\mathrm{pr}}_{X}^{*}\hat{\omega})^{(2,0)_{K\oplus\hat{H}}}
or respectively
{\mathrm{pr}}_{X}^{*}\hat{\underline{\omega}}
is
G
invariant and
K\oplus\hat{H}
horizontal and thus descends to a 2-form
\omega_{\mathbb{M}}
or
\underline{\omega}_{\mathbb{M}}
on
\mathbb{M}
. It is of bidegree
(0,2)
with respect to
\check{H}
and satisfies

\displaystyle{\omega}|_{M_{\zeta}}={\omega}_{\zeta}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{or}\hskip 28.45274pt\underline{\omega}|_{M_{\zeta}}=\underline{\omega}_{\zeta}

•

Let us denote by

\displaystyle\theta_{\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})}}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{1}(\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})},{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{z}^{*})

the soldering form on
\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})}
. Denote
\pi_{\mathfrak{z}}F_{\mathbb{A}}\in\Phi^{(0,2)_{\hat{H}}}(\mathbb{M},\mathfrak{z})
the form defined by

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathfrak{z}}F_{\mathbb{A}}|_{M_{\zeta}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{z}}F_{\mathbb{A}_{\zeta}}.

Then
\mathrm{d}\underline{\omega}_{\mathbb{M}}=\mathrm{d}_{\check{H}}\underline{\omega}_{\mathbb{M}}=-\left<\kappa^{*}\theta_{\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})}}\wedge\pi_{\mathfrak{z}}F_{\mathbb{A}}\right>
respectively.

Beyond infinitesimal variation, it is also natural to ask how different fibres
M_{\zeta}
and
M_{\zeta^{\prime}}
compare globally as metric spaces. Remarkably, in the hyperkähler case, the Gromov-Hausdorff distance between fibres is directly governed by the distance between their parameters. This offers a quantitative perspective on how the quotient spaces degenerate or vary in families.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\in\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{H})}
.The Gromov-Hausdorff distance between
(M_{\zeta},g_{\zeta})
and
(M_{\zeta^{\prime}},g_{\zeta^{\prime}})
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}_{GH}\{(M_{\zeta},g_{\zeta}),(M_{\zeta^{\prime}},g_{\zeta^{\prime}})\}=\left|\zeta-\zeta^{\prime}\right|.

Proof.

The map
\kappa:\mathbb{M}\rightarrow\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})}
defines a Riemannian fibred bundle. We compute

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}_{GH}\{(M_{\zeta},g_{\zeta}),(M_{\zeta^{\prime}},g_{\zeta^{\prime}})\}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\max\{\sup_{m\in M_{\zeta}}\inf_{m^{\prime}\in M_{\zeta^{\prime}}}{\mathrm{d}}_{g_{\mathbb{M}}}(m,m^{\prime}),\sup_{m^{\prime}\in M_{\zeta^{\prime}}}\inf_{m\in M_{\zeta}}{\mathrm{d}}_{g_{\mathbb{M}}}(m,m^{\prime})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}_{\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})}}(\kappa(M_{\zeta}),\kappa(M_{\zeta^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}_{\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})}}(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})

\displaystyle\eqqcolon

\displaystyle|\zeta-\zeta^{\prime}|.

∎

4.4.
Parameter Spaces of Hyperkähler and Calabi-Yau (Q)ALE Spaces

In this section we will summarise the construction of a large class of Ricci-flat (Q)ALE spaces arising as GIT-quotients and (hyper)Kähler quotients. This construction naturally generalises the work of Eguchi-Hansen
[
EH79
]
, Gibbons-Hawking
[
GH78
]
Kronheimer
[
Kro89
,
Kro90
]
in real dimension four as well as the work of Infirri
[
Inf96
]
and Craw-Ishii
[
CI04
]
and others.

This moduli-theoretic viewpoint unifies geometric invariant theory, gauge theory, and derived categories in a natural way. It also lays the groundwork for understanding the geometry of these resolutions via the deformation theory of HYM-connections and the representation theory of
\Gamma
. The following definitions formalise these ideas.

Let in the following
V\cong\mathbb{C}^{m/2}
. As
V
is both an affine variety and a Kähler manifold, complex and symplectic geometry will be used to construct families of resolutions of
V/\Gamma
. Let
(V,\omega_{V},\theta_{V})
be a flat Calabi-Yau structure on
V\cong\mathbb{C}^{m/2}
and let
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{SU}}(V)
preserve
(\omega_{V},\theta_{V})
.

Definition 4.5
.

A
Calabi-Yau (Q)ALE orbifold
, locally modelled on
V/\Gamma
, is given by a noncompact Calabi Yau orbifold
(Z,\omega,\theta)
and a morphism

\displaystyle\rho:(Z,\omega,\theta)\dashrightarrow(V/\Gamma,\omega_{V},\theta_{V})

that restricts to a diffeomorphism
Z\backslash S_{R}\cong V/\Gamma\backslash{\mathrm{Tub}}_{R}(Sing(V/\Gamma))
outside suborbifold
S_{R}
, such that

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{V}})^{k}(\rho_{*}(\omega,\theta)-(\omega_{V},\theta_{V}))=\sum_{0\neq i\in I}\mathcal{O}(d_{i}^{-m-k}).

\lozenge

We will later use techniques from algebraic and symplectic geometry to construct families of Ricci flat Calabi-Yau ALE spaces by resolving quotient singularities. In order to construct such Calabi-Yau metrics on (Q)ALE spaces, the resolutions of
V/\Gamma
need to have a trivial canonical bundle. As
K_{V/\Gamma}
is trivial, we are only interested in resolutions of
V/\Gamma
of the following form.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
(Z,\rho)
be a resolution of a singular variety
Z^{sing}
. The resolution is called
crepant
if
K_{Z}\cong\rho^{*}K_{Z^{sing}}
.

\lozenge

Therefore, we will focus on crepant resolutions of
V/\Gamma
. The topology of these crepant resolutions is totally determined by representations of the isotropy group
\Gamma
as stated in the following conjecture in string theory.

Remark 16
.

Let
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{SU}}(m)
be a finite subgroup, so that the quotient
\mathbb{C}^{m/2}/\Gamma
has Gorenstein singularities. The existence and geometry of crepant resolutions of
\mathbb{C}^{m/2}/\Gamma
depend strongly on the dimension:

•

For
m=2
, these are the classical Kleinian singularities. Crepant resolutions always exist and are unique; they correspond to the minimal resolutions of rational surface singularities.

•

For
m=3
, the singularities are Gorenstein and always admit crepant resolutions, though in general such resolutions are not unique and may be related by flops.

•

For
m=4
, crepant resolutions do not need to exist. Even when they do, they may be non-unique or disconnected in the birational geometry sense.

A remarkable insight from string theory suggests a deep connection between the cohomology of crepant resolutions and the conjugacy classes of the group
\Gamma
. This is captured in the following influential conjecture.

Conjecture 4.1
.

[
Joy00
, Con. 6.4.6]

Let
\Gamma
be a finite subgroup of
{\mathrm{SU}}(m/2)
and
(M,\rho)
a crepant resolution of
V/\Gamma
. There exists a basis of
{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(M,\mathbb{Q})
consisting of algebraic cycles which are in a
correspondence with conjugacy classes of
\Gamma
, such that a conjugacy class of age
k
corresponds to a cycle in
{\mathrm{H}}^{2k}(M,\mathbb{Q})
. In particular,
{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(M;\mathbb{Q})
is concentrated in only even degrees and the Euler characteristic of
M
coincides with the number of conjugacy classes of
\Gamma
.

A partial proof of this conjecture was given in
[
IR96
]
.

Theorem 4.2
.

[
Joy00
, Thm.6.4.7]

Let
\Gamma
be a finite subgroup of
{\mathrm{SL}}(m/2)
and let
(M,\rho)
be a crepant resolution of
V/\Gamma
. Then there is a 1-1 correspondence between compact prime divisors in
M
which form a basis of
{\mathrm{H}}^{2}(M,\mathbb{Q})
and elements of
\Gamma
with age one.

In the following we will use the methods of Section
4.3.1
and
4.3.2
to construct (Q)ALE spaces resolving
\mathbb{C}^{m/2}/\Gamma\cong V/\Gamma
. These spaces arise as moduli spaces of certain symmetric Hermitian Yang-Mills connections on
V
, or by the Uhlenbeck-Yau correspondence
[
UY86
]
as the moduli space of certain “stable” sheaves, generalising the notion of
\Gamma
-orbits. We will follow the work of Infirri
[
Inf96
]
, Kronheimer
[
Kro89
]
, Craw-Ishii
[
CI04
]
, Nakajima
[
Nak94
,
Nak99
]
, Bridgeland-King-Reid
[
BKR01
]
and Yamagishi
[
Yam23
,
Yam25
]
. In physics the following construction is referred to as the construction of the moduli spaces of D0 branes on
V/\Gamma
(see
[
DM96
,
DGM97
]
).

These moduli spaces will be equipped with (hyper)Kähler ALE metrics. In certain cases Joyce proof of the ALE-Calabi Conjecture
[
Joy00
, Thm. 8.5.1]
ensures the existence of a Ricci-flat Calabi-Yau metric.

To make these constructions explicit, we now turn to the quiver-theoretic perspective. The action of
\Gamma
on
V
gives rise to a quiver whose representation theory encodes the geometry of the quotient singularity and its resolutions.

Notation
.

Let in the following
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{SU}}(V)
be a finite subgroup,
{\mathrm{Irr}}(\Gamma)
denotes the set of irreducible representations of
\Gamma
,
{\mathrm{Rep}}(\Gamma)
its representation ring and
{\mathrm{R}}
its regular representation. The space

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathbb{Z}}=\{\theta_{\zeta}\in{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathrm{Rep}}(\Gamma),\mathbb{Z})|\theta_{\zeta}({\mathrm{R}})=0\}

will be referred to as the space of integral stability condition or as the parameter space. Set
\Theta_{\mathbb{Q}}=\Theta_{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
,
\Theta_{\mathbb{R}}=\Theta_{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\cong\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})}
and
\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{H})}=\Theta_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{H})
.

Remark 17
.

Notice that an integral stability condition determines a character of
\Gamma
by

\displaystyle\chi_{\zeta}([g])=\prod_{\varrho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(\Gamma)}{\mathrm{det}}(g_{\varrho})^{\theta_{\zeta}(g_{\varrho})}.

There exists a set of “bad” stability conditions

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}\subset\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})},

formed by the
{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{K})
-span of the integral stability conditions satisfying

\displaystyle\sum_{\varrho\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(\Gamma)}a_{\varrho}\theta_{\varrho}=0,

where the
a_{\varrho}\in\{0,1,...,{\mathrm{dim}}_{\mathbb{C}}(R_{\varrho})\}
. The polyhedral cone formed by these “bad” stability conditions will be referred to as the walls, and its complement as the chambers of the GIT-quotient.

We begin by recalling the construction of the McKay quiver (see
[
Rei02
]
) associated to a finite subgroup
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SU}(V)
. This extends Theorem
4.5
.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SU}(V)
be a finite subgroup. The associated
McKay quiver

Q_{\Gamma}
is defined as follows:

•

The set of vertices
Q_{\Gamma}^{0}
is given by the isomorphism classes of irreducible representations
\varrho\in\mathrm{Irr}(\Gamma)
.

•

The number of arrows from
\varrho
to
\sigma
is determined by the multiplicities
a_{\varrho\sigma}
in the decomposition

\displaystyle V\otimes R_{\varrho}\cong\bigoplus_{\sigma\in Q_{\Gamma}^{0}}a_{\varrho\sigma}R_{\sigma}.

\lozenge

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1}}

Figure 4.
McKay Quiver of the group
D_{4}\subset{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
and the group
\mathbb{Z}_{3}\subset{\mathrm{SU}}(3)
.

Given the dimension vector
\vec{v}=(\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\varrho_{0},\dots,\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\varrho_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{\Gamma}^{0}}
and a framing vector
\vec{w}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{\Gamma}^{0}}
, we define the framed representation space

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Rep}}(Q_{\Gamma},\vec{v}\oplus\vec{w})\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\varrho\sigma\in Q_{\Gamma}^{1}}\mathrm{Hom}(R_{\varrho},R_{\sigma})\oplus\bigoplus_{\tau\in Q_{\Gamma}^{0}}\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{w_{\tau}},R_{\tau}),

on which a product of general linear groups acts. This action factors through the projective groups

\displaystyle\mathrm{PGL}(\vec{v})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\prod_{\varrho\in Q_{\Gamma}^{0}}\mathrm{GL}(v_{\varrho})\right)\big/\mathbb{C}^{\times},

\displaystyle\mathrm{PU}(\vec{v})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\prod_{\varrho\in Q_{\Gamma}^{0}}\mathrm{U}(v_{\varrho})\right)\big/\mathrm{U}(1).

Remark 18
.

The regular representation can be identified with representation

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\cong{\mathrm{Rep}}(Q_{\Gamma},0\oplus\vec{v})=\bigoplus_{Q_{\Gamma}^{0}}{\mathrm{Hom}}(\mathbb{C}^{{\mathrm{dim}}_{\mathbb{C}}(\varrho)},R_{\varrho}).

The representation-theoretic data admits a natural interpretation in terms of symplectic geometry. In particular, the group
\mathrm{PU}(\vec{v})
acts in a Hamiltonian way on
{\mathrm{Rep}}(Q_{\Gamma},\vec{v}\oplus\vec{w})

[
Kin94
,
Nak94
]
.

One of the central insights in this construction is that the quotient singularity
V/\Gamma
can be resolved via moduli spaces of suitably framed sheaves or representations, where the regular representation
{\mathrm{R}}
plays a central role. The key idea is to reinterpret points in
V/\Gamma
as corresponding to “trivial” orbits of
\Gamma
, and to generalise this correspondence by considering more flexible structures, namely,
\Gamma
-equivariant coherent sheaves on
V
whose global sections realise the regular representation. These sheaves, known as
\Gamma
-constellations, will serve as the fundamental objects parametrised by the moduli spaces that resolve
V/\Gamma
.

We can describe
\Gamma
-constellations in terms of explicit linear algebra data associated to quiver representations. This leads to a concrete algebraic description of the moduli space of such sheaves.

Definition 4.8
.

A
\Gamma
-constellation
is a coherent,
\Gamma
-equivariant sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
V
, such that
{\mathrm{H}}^{0}(V,\mathcal{F})={\mathrm{R}}
as
\Gamma
-representations.

\lozenge

Remark 19
.

A
\Gamma
-constellation is a finite union of
\Gamma
-orbits and therefore sheaf-theoretic generalisations of
\Gamma
-orbits. Further, notice that a
\Gamma
-constellation on
V
corresponds to a
\Gamma
-equivariant
{\mathrm{Sym}}^{\bullet}V^{*}
-module structure on
{\mathrm{R}}
.

Lemma 4.9
.

We can identify the space of
\Gamma
-constellations on
V
with the space

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{0}=\left\{\left.A\in{\mathrm{Rep}}(Q_{\Gamma},\vec{v})\right|[A\wedge A]=0\in{\mathrm{End}}({\mathrm{R}})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\wedge^{2}V\right\}\subset{\mathrm{Rep}}(Q_{\Gamma},\vec{v}).

Moreover, this space is an affine Kähler variety.

Proof.

We identify the two spaces by sending a
A\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
to the
\Gamma
-constellation corresponding to
ev_{A}\colon{\mathrm{Sym}}^{\bullet}V^{*}\rightarrow{\mathrm{End}}(R)
. Notice that this space is an affine complex variety of
{\mathrm{Rep}}(Q_{\Gamma},\vec{v})
and thus inherits its Kähler structure.
∎

The Hermitian structure on
{\mathrm{R}}
induces one on
{\mathrm{End}}(R)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V
. We will use the Uhlenbeck-Yau correspondence to construct a Kähler structure on the moduli space of
\Gamma
-constellations.

The space of
V
-translational invariant Hermitian connections on the trivial
{\mathrm{R}}
-bundle over
V
can be identified with the space

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Rep}}(Q_{\Gamma},\vec{v})\coloneqq({\mathrm{End}}({\mathrm{R}})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V)^{\Gamma}\cong(\mathfrak{u}({\mathrm{R}})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}V)^{\Gamma}.

With this linear algebraic setup, we are now ready to describe the moduli space of constellations as a symplectic or Kähler quotient. The following proposition summarises this correspondence in terms of moment maps and stability parameters.

The following Proposition summarises the results for
m=4
and
m=6
.

Proposition 4.6
.

[
Kro89
, Prop. 2.1, (2.7)]
[
Inf96
, Sec. 3.4]
[
Nak94
, Thm. 1.3]

The group
{\mathrm{PU}}(\vec{v})
acts on
{\mathrm{Rep}}(Q_{\Gamma},\vec{v}\oplus\vec{w})
in a Hamiltonian way with moment map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{Q_{\Gamma},\vec{v}\oplus\vec{w}}:{\mathrm{Rep}}(Q_{\Gamma},\vec{v}\oplus\vec{w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{pu}(\vec{v})^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A,B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum_{\sigma\varrho\in Q^{1}_{\Gamma}}A_{\varrho}A_{\varrho}^{\dagger}-\sum_{\varrho\sigma\in Q^{1}_{\Gamma}}A^{\dagger}_{\varrho}A_{\varrho}+B_{\sigma}B_{\sigma}^{\dagger}.}

Furthermore, we can identify the space of real stability conditions
13
13

13

The Kähler moment map is valued in
{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})^{*}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.

\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})}
with the centre
\mathfrak{z}^{*}(\mathfrak{pu}(\vec{v}))

14
14

14

Here
\mathfrak{z}^{*}(\mathfrak{pu}(\vec{v}))\coloneqq{\mathrm{Ann}}([\mathfrak{pu}(\vec{v}),\mathfrak{pu}(\vec{v})])
.
via

\displaystyle\zeta\mapsto\left(\xi\mapsto\theta_{\zeta}(\xi):=-\sum_{\varrho\in Q^{0}_{\Gamma}}\zeta(\varrho){\mathrm{tr}}(\xi\cdot\pi_{\varrho})\right)

Here
\pi_{\varrho}:R\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{{\mathrm{dim}}\varrho}\otimes R^{\varrho}
is the projection of the regular representation onto its
\varrho
-isotypical component understood as an element of
{\mathrm{End}}(R)
. Moreover, the Kähler quotient and the GIT quotient

\displaystyle M_{\zeta}=\left(\mu^{-1}_{Q_{\Gamma},\vec{v}\oplus 0}(\zeta)\cap\mathcal{N}_{0}\right)/{\mathrm{PU}}(\vec{v})\cong\mathcal{N}_{0}\sslash_{\zeta}{\mathrm{PGL}}(\vec{v})

coincide, whereby
\rho_{\zeta}:M_{\zeta}\dashrightarrow V/\Gamma
is a crepant resolution, and
M_{\zeta}
is smooth for all
\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})}\backslash\mathcal{W}
.

Figure 5.
Parameter space of resolutions of
V/\Gamma
.

Corollary 4.2
.

There exists a stratified fibration

\displaystyle\kappa:\mathbb{M}\rightarrow\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})}

and a vertical Kähler form
\omega_{\mathbb{M}}\in\Omega^{0,2}(\mathbb{M})
such that

\displaystyle\rho_{\zeta}:(\kappa^{-1}(\zeta),\omega_{\mathbb{M}}|_{\kappa^{-1}(\zeta)})=(M_{\zeta},\omega_{\zeta})\dashrightarrow(V/\Gamma,\omega_{V})

The birational map
\rho_{\zeta}:M_{\zeta}\dashrightarrow V/\Gamma
can be geometrically described as the map

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathbb{M}}\colon\mathbb{M}\rightarrow V/\Gamma,m\mapsto{\mathrm{argmin}}_{x\in V/\Gamma}{\mathrm{dist}}_{g_{\mathbb{M}}}(m,x).

As the spaces
M_{\zeta}
can be viewed as moduli spaces of symmetric HYM-connections on
V
, we have a nice local description of the tangent space as the cohomology of the deformation complex of the HYM-equations. Moreover, Infirri
[
Inf96
]
used Kuranshi-germs to describe the space
M_{\zeta}
locally.

The local structure of these moduli spaces can be understood via deformation theory. In particular, the Zariski tangent space and singularities of
M_{\zeta}
can be described using a differential graded Lie algebra associated to the Hermitian Yang–Mills complex.

Proposition 4.7
.

[
Inf96
, Thm. 6.1]

Let
M^{\bullet,\bullet}=\wedge^{\bullet,\bullet}V^{*}\otimes{\mathrm{End}}({\mathrm{R}})
and define

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}_{\alpha}:M^{p,q}\rightarrow M^{p,q+1},\hskip 28.45274pt\beta\mapsto[\alpha\wedge\beta].

If
\alpha\in N_{0}
then
(M^{0,\bullet},\overline{\partial}_{\alpha})
is a differential complex. As
{\mathrm{End}}(R)
carries an inner product we obtain a Hodge-decomposition of
(M^{0,\bullet},\overline{\partial}_{\alpha})
by

\displaystyle M^{0,p}=\overline{\partial}_{\alpha}M^{0,p-1}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{0,p}_{\alpha}\oplus\overline{\partial}^{*}_{\alpha}M^{0,p+1}.

and

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}^{*}_{\alpha}\beta=-\Lambda[\overline{\alpha},\beta]

where
\Lambda:M^{p,q}\rightarrow M^{p-1,q-1}
is the Lefschetz operator with respect to the Kähler form on
S
. The Zariski tangent space of
M_{\zeta}
at
[\alpha]
can be identified with

\displaystyle T_{[\alpha]}M_{\zeta}\cong(\mathcal{H}^{0,1}_{\alpha})^{\Gamma}.

If
[\alpha]
has trivial isotropy, then
M_{\zeta}
is locally isomorphic to its Kuranishi germ

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}\coloneqq\{\beta\in(M^{0,1})^{\Gamma}|\overline{\partial}^{*}_{\alpha}\beta=0,\overline{\partial}_{\alpha}\beta+\frac{1}{2}[\beta,\beta]=0\}.

Using this local description of
M_{\zeta}
in terms of its Kuranishi germ,
[
Inf96
]
formulated the following conjecture on the behaviour of the possible singularities appearing in
M_{\zeta}
.

Conjecture 4.2
.

[
Inf96
, Con. 1]

The differential graded Lie algebra
((M^{0,\bullet})^{\Gamma},\overline{\partial}_{\alpha})
is formal for all
\alpha\in\mathcal{N}_{0}\cap\mu^{-1}(\zeta)
and generic
\zeta
, therefore
M_{\zeta}
has, for these
\zeta
, at worst quadratic algebraic singularities.

The construction of
M_{\zeta}
as a moduli space of
\Gamma
-constellations not only provides a geometric resolution of the singular quotient
V/\Gamma
, but also encodes deep algebraic information about the original orbifold structure. A particularly striking consequence of this perspective is the existence of a derived equivalence between the resolved space and the category of
\Gamma
-equivariant sheaves on the smooth variety
V
. This result, in the spirit of the classical McKay correspondence, reveals that the geometry of
M_{\zeta}
reflects representation-theoretic data in a precise and categorical way.

This relationship is formalised by a derived equivalence between the category of coherent sheaves on the resolved space and the category of coherent
\Gamma
-equivariant sheaves on
V
. Known as the derived McKay correspondence, this theorem shows the deep connection between the geometry of the resolution and the representation theory of the orbifold group. The equivalence is realised via a Fourier–Mukai transform, whose kernel is the universal
\Gamma
-constellation, thereby intertwining geometric structures on the resolution with representation-theoretic data encoded in the action of
\Gamma
.

Theorem 4.3
.

[
CI04
, Thm. 2.5]
[
BKR01
, Thm. 1.2]

There exists an equivalence of categories

\displaystyle\Psi_{\zeta}\colon\textbf{D}^{b}(M_{\zeta})\rightarrow\textbf{D}^{b}(V)^{\Gamma}

of the bounded derived category of coherent sheaves on
M_{\zeta}
and the bounded derived category of coherent
\Gamma
-sheaves on
V
.

Proof.

The equivalence
\Psi_{\zeta}=\mathbb{R}(\pi_{V})_{*}(\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}\otimes\pi_{M_{\zeta}}^{*}(-\otimes R_{0}))
is given by the Fourier-Mukai transform with kernel
\mathcal{U}_{\zeta}
, the universal
\Gamma
-constellation on
M_{\zeta}\times V
, with respect to the stability condition
\zeta\in\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})}
.
∎

By applying
[
CI04
, Thm. 2.5]
Craw and Ishii were able to deduce the following.

Corollary 4.3
.

[
CI04
, Prop. 2.2]

There exists a family of tautological vector bundles over
M_{\zeta}

\displaystyle R_{\varrho}=\mu^{-1}(\zeta)\cap\mathcal{N}_{0}\times_{{\mathrm{PU}}(\vec{v})}{\mathrm{R}}_{\varrho}.

The family
R_{\varrho}
forms a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis in the
K
-theory of
M_{\zeta}
and thus their Chern-characters form a basis of
{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(M_{\zeta})
.

The dependence of the moduli space
M_{\zeta}
on the chosen stability parameter
\zeta\in\Theta_{\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{C})}
is a rich source of geometric structure. Varying
\zeta
deforms the resolution and can lead to birational transformations between different crepant resolutions of
V/\Gamma
reflecting changes in the GIT quotient structure. These transformations are governed by a wall-and-chamber decomposition of the stability space, where crossing a wall corresponds to a qualitative change in the geometry of
M_{\zeta}
.

Understanding this variation not only sheds light on the birational geometry of
M_{\zeta}
, but also on the symmetries and derived equivalences between different moduli spaces. In particular, symmetries of the group
\Gamma
and its normaliser in
{\mathrm{SO}}(V)
induce automorphisms of the universal moduli space
\mathbb{M}\rightarrow\Theta_{\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{C})}
, intertwining different resolutions in a structured way. If
\zeta
varies across walls in the space of stability parameters, the geometry of
M_{\zeta}
can undergo dramatic changes. We now explore this wall-crossing behaviour in detail.

Corollary 4.4
.

[
CI04
, Lem. 3.1]

There exists a universal family of Kähler quotients
\kappa\colon\mathbb{M}\rightarrow\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})}
with Ehresmann connection as constructed in the Section
4.3.2
such that the fibres are smooth away from codimension one walls
\mathcal{W}\subset\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})}
.

In addition to the wall-and-chamber decomposition, the universal bundle admits a rich symmetry structure. These symmetries, arising from the representation theory of
\Gamma
, act on the stability space and intertwine different fibres of the moduli space.

Lemma 4.10
.

[
CI04
, Lem. 2.6]

Let
\mathbb{W}
denote the subgroup of
{\mathrm{GL}}(\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})})
generated by all one dimensional
\sigma\in{\mathrm{Irr}}(\Gamma)
acting by

\displaystyle\sigma.\theta_{\zeta}(\varrho)=\theta_{\zeta}(\sigma\otimes\varrho)

and by

\displaystyle\theta_{\zeta}^{*}(\varrho)=-\theta_{\zeta}(\varrho^{*}).

The universal bundle
\kappa\colon\mathbb{M}\rightarrow\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})}
is
\mathbb{W}
-equivariant.

These symmetries not only act on the total space of the bundle but also control how the geometry of the resolution changes as
\zeta
crosses a wall. The types of singular transitions that may occur have been classified into a small number of wall-crossing phenomena.

Lemma 4.11
.

[

, Prop. 3.8]

Let

or

. There exist three types of wall-crossing phenomena on
\kappa\colon\mathbb{M}\rightarrow\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})}
. Let

be a smooth path such that
\zeta((0,\epsilon))\in\Theta^{reg}_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})}
and
0\neq\theta(0)\in\mathcal{W}

0.

The isomorphism type of
M_{\zeta}
does not change along
\zeta

I.

A curve in
M_{\zeta}
is contracted to a point.

III.

A divisor in
M_{\zeta}
is contracted to a curve.

This classification provides a concrete description of how birational modifications, such as flops, emerge from wall-crossing. In most cases, the geometry undergoes controlled contractions of subvarieties, leading to birational models that remain crepant resolutions.

Remark 20
.

Let
\zeta:[0,\epsilon)\rightarrow\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})}
be smooth path such that
\zeta((0,\epsilon))\in\Theta^{reg}_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})}
and
0\neq\theta(0)\in\mathcal{W}
. Then it is expected that either the isomorphism type of
M_{\zeta}
does not change, or a dimension
d
subscheme is contracted onto a dimension
d-1
subscheme.

To fully capture the symmetries of the moduli problem, one considers an extended symmetry group acting on both the parameter space and the universal bundle. This larger group includes the representation-theoretic symmetries and may include additional geometric automorphisms.

Remark 21
.

There exists possibly a larger group of automorphisms of the universal bundle. This group
\mathbb{U}
satisfies

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{U}}

In fact, a significant portion of the symmetry group

With these symmetries and the wall-crossing structure in place, we can now focus on a particularly tractable and important case, when
\Gamma
acts freely away from the origin, i.e.

is an orbifold with a single singular stratum
[0/\Gamma]
. In this situation, the geometry of the resolution simplifies considerably, as the exceptional set is compact, and the space is asymptotically modelled on the smooth quotient
(V\backslash\{0\})/\Gamma
. We begin by describing the global topological and asymptotic structure of the resolved space.

Lemma 4.12
.

[

, Thm. 8.4.1]

The exceptional divisor
\Upsilon_{\zeta}
of the resolution

and

, and

\displaystyle{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(M_{\zeta})={\mathrm{H}}^{k}_{c}(M_{\zeta})={\mathrm{H}}^{k}_{sing}(M_{\zeta}).

for

.

We will now summarize the results of
[

]

[

Proposition 4.8
.

[
Kro89
, Prop. 3.14]

The quotient metric

is ALE of rate
-4
, i.e.

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{0}})^{k}\left((\rho_{\zeta})_{*}g_{\zeta}-g_{0}\right)=\mathcal{O}(r^{-4-k}).

Remark 22
.

Kronheimer argued that there exists a asymptotic expansion of the Kähler metric
g_{\zeta}
. By Section
4.3.2
we know that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\omega_{\mathbb{M}}={\mathrm{d}}_{\check{H}}\omega_{\mathbb{M}}=\left<\kappa^{*}\theta_{\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{C})}}\wedge\pi_{\mathfrak{z}}F_{\mathbb{A}}\right>

and as

we deduced that the second order in the asymptotic expansion vanishes, thus

is ALE of rate
-4
.

While the existence of an ALE metric with controlled decay is already a powerful geometric result, our broader goal is to obtain Ricci-flat metrics. This motivates the following conjecture, which asserts the existence of ALE Calabi–Yau structures on these spaces in dimensions
m=4
and
m=6
.

Conjecture 4.3
.

The ALE space
(M_{\zeta},\omega_{\zeta})
is a Calabi-Yau ALE space of rate
-m
.

Assuming Conjecture

Conjecture 4.4

The connection

restricts to fibrewise Hermitian Yang-Mills connections on

to the flat connection. Furthermore, this connection is irreducible and unobstructed.

From the previous section we know that for all

is a smooth Kähler manifold. By Corollary

In particular, the action of

. Notice that the moment map is quadratic under scaling, i.e

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{t}\colon\mu^{-1}(\zeta)}

.

Furthermore, by descending to

\displaystyle\delta_{t}^{*}\omega_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}=t^{2}\cdot\omega_{\zeta}

Geometrically, the map

4.4.1.
Parameter Space of Hyperkähler ALE Four Manifolds

We now focus on four-dimensional hyperkähler ALE manifolds, corresponding to resolutions of singularities of the form
\mathbb{H}/\Gamma
, where
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SU}(2)\cong\mathrm{Sp}(1)
is a finite subgroup. This classical situation, studied extensively by Kronheimer
[
Kro89
]
, exhibits a rich interplay between quiver varieties, hyperkähler geometry, and representation theory. The constructions that follow illustrate how hyperkähler structures arise naturally from the symplectic geometry of representation spaces associated to the McKay quiver.

Let in the following
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{Sp}}(1)\cong{\mathrm{SU}}(2)
be a finite subgroup acting canonically on
\mathbb{H}
. The space

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Rep}}(Q_{\Gamma},\vec{v})\cong(\mathbb{H}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathrm{End}}({\mathrm{R}}))^{\Gamma}\cong(\mathbb{H}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathfrak{u}({\mathrm{R}}))^{\Gamma}

naturally carries a hyperkähler structure. This hyperkähler structure is induced by

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto(-\beta^{*},\alpha^{*}).

Here
\alpha\mapsto\alpha^{*}
denote the real structure induced by the choice of a Hermitian product on
{\mathrm{R}}
. The group
{\mathrm{PU}}(\vec{v})\cong{\mathrm{PU}}({\mathrm{R}})^{\Gamma}
acts on
{\mathrm{Rep}}(Q_{\Gamma},\vec{v})
in a trihamiltonian way. By identifying
\mathfrak{pu}({\mathrm{R}})^{*}
with traceless elements of
\mathfrak{u}(R)
and by picking a basis
\alpha,\beta
of
\mathbb{C}^{2}\cong\mathbb{H}
, we can write down the hyperkähler moment map explicitly

\displaystyle\mu_{Q_{\Gamma},\vec{v}}(\alpha,\beta)=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}i([\alpha,\alpha^{*}]+[\beta,\beta^{*}])\\
([\alpha,\beta]+[\alpha^{*},\beta^{*}])\\
i(-[\alpha,\beta]+[\alpha^{*},\beta^{*}])\quad.\end{array}\right)

The moment map
\mu_{Q_{\Gamma},\vec{v}}=\mu_{\mathbb{R}}+\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
splits into a real and into a complex part with respect to the natural complex structure associated to the Kähler structure discussed in the previous section.

Remark 23
.

The sets
\mu^{-1}_{\mathbb{C}}(0)
and
\mathcal{N}_{0}
coincide.

Kronheimer showed that the hyperkähler quotients are complete hyperkähler orbifolds asymptotic to
(\mathbb{H}/\Gamma,\underline{\omega}_{0})
.

Theorem 4.4
.

[
Kro89
, Thm. 1.1]

For each
\zeta\in\Theta_{{\mathrm{Im}}(\mathbb{H})}
, the space

\displaystyle(M_{\zeta},\underline{\omega}_{\zeta})=(\mu^{-1}(\zeta),\underline{\omega})/{\mathrm{PU}}(\vec{v})

is a hyperkähler ALE-space rate
-4
. Moreover,
\mathbb{A}_{\zeta}
is an ASD instanton. Hence, both conjectures
4.3
and
4.4
hold for
m=4
.

The McKay correspondence for finite groups
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{Sp}}(1)
relates the structure of the McKay quiver to simply-laced ADE Dynkin diagrams.

Theorem 4.5
.

[
McK80
]

The quiver
Q_{\Gamma}
can be identified with a simply laced extended Dynkin-diagram of ADE type. In particular, let
A
be the matrix, given by the
a_{\varrho\sigma}\in Q_{\Gamma}^{1}
, which itself are either zero or one. Then
\overline{C}=2I-A
is a Cartan matrix.
